# Consideration of funding proposals - Addendum IX

## Funding proposal package for FP267

### Summary

This addendum contains the following seven parts:

a) A funding proposal titled "Scaling up ecosystem-based approaches to managing climate-related and classified disaster risks in vulnerable regions of South Africa ("Eco-DRR");"

b) No objection letter issued by the national designated authority(ies) or focal point(s);

c) Environmental and social aspect(s) disclosure;

d) Sectoral assessment;

e) Independent Technical Advisory Panel's assessment;

f) Response from the accredited entity to the independent Technical Advisory Panel's assessment;

g) Gender documentation.

This document is prepared and transmitted for consideration by the GCF Board at a Board meeting. It is published on the GCF public website in its current, unedited form. The GCF Board may make changes to this document and updates to it if any will be made publicly available in accordance with applicable GCF policies.

## Note to Accredited Entities on the use of the funding proposal template

* Accredited Entities should provide summary information in the proposal with cross-reference to annexes.
* Accredited Entities should ensure that annexes provided are consistent with the details provided in the narrative sections and vice versa. This will be checked by GCF during the review of the documents.
* The total number of pages for the funding proposal (excluding annexes) should not exceed 60.
* Proposals exceeding the prescribed length will not be assessed within the usual service standard time.
* Under the GCF Information Disclosure Policy, project and programme funding proposals will be disclosed to the public. Accredited Entities are requested to ensure that they do not include any information that may be disclosed pursuant to the IDP. Accredited Entities are asked to fill out their information on disclosure.

## A.21. Executive summary (max 750 words, approximately 1.5 pages)

The intensity and frequency of droughts, extreme rainfall events and wildfires across South Africa have increased significantly in recent years. This has led to a shift from the current water management model that treats these trends as an anomaly towards a new water management model which identifies these trends as ongoing and persistent. This will likely affect livelihoods, food and water security as well as ecosystem services.

### 2024:

- **Prolonged climate hazards include damage to both built and natural infrastructure and impacts on local livelihoods, including reduced availability of water for both domestic and agricultural use, decreased crop yields, increased soil erosion, and loss of biodiversity. These factors will likely lead to a reduction in buffering of food and drinking impacts by the natural environment. Climate change will exacerbate these impacts on already stressed ecosystem goods and services that underpin social and economic development through a range of pathways including reduced water availability, increased frequency and intensity of extreme weather events leading to damage to ecological infrastructure and in livelihood insecurity.

- **Current climate adaptation efforts are fragmented and lack coordination among government entities and communities. As such, the types of approaches and interventions needed to support climate resilience are not being implemented at scale due to a lack of political will and institutional capacity for response due to a lack of a strong evidence base of the benefits that would be realised. In addition, institutional complexity and fragmentation across levels of governance (national, provincial, district, local) leads to poor coordination, resulting in fragmented implementation of climate change adaptation efforts (Cerra, 2013; Adom et al., 2015). This is further exacerbated by the lack of a clear mandate at national level for climate change and limited financial mandate at the local level. Limited number of climate change staff at district and local levels, lack of comprehensive planning processes that integrate climate change adaptation into development plans, inadequate integrated development plans, insufficient financial resources, inadequate human capacity at the provincial and local levels, and poor understanding of climate change adaptation issues by communities (Sibaya et al., 2020).

- **Well-functioning ecosystems are critical for supporting livelihoods through food and water security, safety, health and well-being. Ecosystem-based adaptation (EBA) and ecosystem-based disaster risk reduction (Eco-DRR) draw from practices like conservation and restoration of wetlands to improve the resilience of communities against climate change impacts by combining the combination of restoration, conservation, sustainable use and management of wetlands, land and other natural

## The project proposes to address four barriers that constrain the achievement of the permanent solution adaptation.

### Barriers to effective participation and community engagement and agency for addressing climate change-induced disasters.
- Barrier: Lack of capacity, resources, and lack of a participatory community engagement and agency for addressing climate change-induced disasters.
- Barrier: Limited access to resources and lack of an evidence base to catalyse investment in ecological infrastructure for Eco-DRR.

### Barriers to the full integration of ECO-DRR into natural resource management and disaster risk reduction (DRR) interventions.

- Barrier: Limited access to resources and lack of an evidence base to catalyse investment in ecological infrastructure for Eco-DRR.

## B. PROJECT/PROGRAMME INFORMATION

### B.1. Project context (max. 1000 words, approximately 2 pages)

#### National Climate Context

South Africa experiences monthly temperatures ranging from 15–38°C in summer and 2–26°C in winter (World Bank, 2021). Rainfall over South Africa is highly variable, with the west of the country being dry and hyper-arid and the east experiencing high rainfall. The country has experienced a range of extreme weather events including droughts, floods, and heatwaves. There have been records of hydrometeorological events – notably high temperature events, floods and droughts - have been recorded in recent years (e.g., 2015/2016 drought; 2018/2019 drought; 2020/2021 drought; 2021/2022 drought; and the floods in KwaZulu-Natal (FDCR, 2024)). Fire plays an important role in South African ecosystems. The expansion of fire-prone land into areas that were previously not exposed to fire at the boundary between developed land and fire-prone vegetation, communities are becoming increasingly exposed to wildfire threat. There is also a demonstrable link between fire hazard and the invasion of alien plants (APA) (FCSR, 2019).

Climate change models indicate that these trends will continue and accelerate. In addition to this, climate change is expected to increase the vulnerability of communities to damage to both built and ecological infrastructure: impacts on local livelihoods, including reduced availability of water resources; increased risk of fire weather events; increased soil erosion; increased salinity due to irrigation and salt nutrient leaching; and reduced buffering from the natural environment of flood and drought impacts. These changes will have a significant impact on rural communities as they are already vulnerable and underpin social and economic development through a range of pathways. # GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 9

## These climate change impacts result in water insecurity, damage to ecological infrastructure and in livelihood insecurity (Figure 2).

### 16. Climate change is causing several ongoing anthropogenic impacts, including inter alia overgrazing, the rapid spread of IAPs, soil compaction and the removal of natural vegetation cover, exacerbating the cycle of land degradation and poverty across large parts of rural South Africa. Due to women's strong reliance on natural resources for their livelihoods, they are disproportionately affected by climate change impacts such as the loss of biodiversity which forces women to spend more time and walk greater distances to collect fuel, water and food (Mokgatle et al., 2014).

### Similarly, youth are just as vulnerable to degradation and climate change as current and future generations depend on common property resources for their livelihoods. The majority of the rural population in South Africa is young and particularly are affected by climate induced displacement and migration which disrupts education and community cohesion (Mokgatle et al., 2014). This is further exacerbated by the loss of traditional land tenure systems, including customary land rights, which are being eroded as a result of the commonwealth. (2014). Continental studies have shown that as high as 95% of 20 migrants are under the age of 19 with climate change being one of the major reasons for migration (Mokgatle et al., 2014).

### The increased intensity of flood, drought and wildfire cycles is particularly concerning given soil and erosion by water or wind. This has been exacerbated in recent years due to climate change and land use change (Liu et al., 2016) because of the interconnected effects of land use and climate change (EA, Borelli, et al., 2020). This increased intensity of flood, drought and wildfire cycles is also linked with the increasing frequency of extreme weather events as agriculture is practiced. (Compton, et al., 2016). This not only impacts on rangeland quality but also impacts the functioning of ecological infrastructure.

### Current climate change adaptation policy in South Africa has a limited understanding of the value and benefits accrued from climate change adaptation response among government entities, the private sector and communities. As such, the types of ecosystem services provided by ecosystems are not fully recognized as an important component for climate change investment support. This is primarily due to lack of discourse around climate change adaptation due to the lack of a shared understanding of the value and benefits accrued from climate change adaptation response. Limited intergovernmental coordination, poorly defined legal mandates at the local level, limited number of climate change adaptation projects in South Africa, poor integration of climate change adaptation into development plans (absence or) information on climate change to inform integrated development plans, insufficient financial resources, inadequate capacity and skills for climate change adaptation planning at national and local levels, and a poor understanding of climate change adaptation issues by communities. (Silvay, et al., 2014).

## Preferred Solution

### 20. Ecosystems and ecological infrastructure can play a key role in achieving positive social and development outcomes, by enhancing food and water security, safety, health and well-being. “Ecosystem-based adaptation” is an approach that integrates the conservation of ecosystems with human activities to enhance the conservation and ecosystem restoration to enhance ecosystem resilience. The South African Strategic Framework on Ecosystem-Based Adaptation (SASFA) was developed in 2013 as a national framework for integrated climate change adaptation response. The national position on EBA sets out to integrate ecosystem services into development planning, policy making and decision-making processes at all levels of government and explicitly emphasises human well-being and adaptation on benefits of healthy ecosystems linking these to long-term socio-economic development.

### 21. Both EBA and Eco-DRR approaches (such as the combination of restoration, conservation, sustainable use and management of wetlands, land and other natural resources with disaster risk reduction (DRR) interventions) are a key approach for climate change adaptation in South Africa due to their ability to provide multiple benefits to people, ecosystems, and biodiversity. They can be combined with other development approaches such as integrated water resource management, sustainable land use development options due to their ability to offer multiple benefits to people, ecosystems, and biodiversity. They can be combined with other development approaches such as integrated water resource management, sustainable land use development options due to their ability to offer multiple benefits to people, ecosystems, and biodiversity. Therefore, it is recommended that the South African government should continue to support and promote EBA and Eco-DRR approaches at the national and sub-national level in South Africa, and be adequately resourced so that climate benefits are maximized.

### 22 The Eco-DRR project will aim to scale up EBA and Eco-DRR approaches for the management of climate intensified disaster risks, in targeted regions of South Africa. The mainstreaming of these approaches will enhance the

## Barriers to the preferred solution

The following are four barriers that constrain the achievement of the preferred adaptation solution:

### Barrier 1: Challenges within the enabling environment regarding collaboration, coordination, capacity and integration of Eco-DRR into existing national, provincial or local competencies.

Institutional complexity between the spheres of government contributes to suboptimal intergovernmental and cross-sectoral coordination, resulting in fragmented implementation of Eco-DRR initiatives. The lack of a clear mandate for the Department of Environmental Affairs (DEA) to update and upskill new innovative business proposals are possibly key drivers. The project will undertake interventions to engage with youth and to develop Eco-DRR related opportunities that will meet their social and economic needs in this regard.

### Barrier 2: Limited implementation of EEA and Eco-DRR at scale

Many of the EEA and Eco-DRR interventions implemented by government have not been scaled up, resulting in limited impact on poverty reduction and job creation. As a result, the positive impact of these measures has not been well documented and the impact will undermine.

### Barrier 3: Limited implementation of Eco-DRR at scale

The project will develop an enabling environment for the development of an ecological infrastructure for Eco-DRR. The absence of implementation at scale and a comprehensive documented evidence base that can demonstrate the positive impacts of these measures on poverty reduction, job creation and resilience into this programme of work.

### Barrier 4: Limited appreciation of the potential for public sector investment further hinder resource mobilisation efforts

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 11

If ecosystem-based approaches are adopted as a strategic measure to mitigate the risks posed by climate change, then the resilience of ecological infrastructure and communities that are vulnerable to climate change will be enhanced.

Because transformed landscape management practices reduce disaster preparedness and response, and sustainable investment in Eco-DRR will have resulted in access to the benefits of ecosystem services that enhance community livelihoods, this goal has been achieved.

32. To achieve this goal statement, the Eco DRR project has been structured under three outcomes:

* **Outcome 1** will support the incorporation of Eco-DRR strategies into integrated landscape management through a participatory and adaptive approach to improve ecological infrastructure and communities through a site-based programme of work that improves ecosystem condition and supports local led adaptation.
* **Outcome 2** will support the development, implementation, and institutionalization of climate change response and the mainstreaming of Eco-DRR approaches into asset risk management, environmental policy and spatial planning.
* **Outcome 3** the project will overcome barriers by creating an enabling environment for investment in Eco-DRR through the provision of evidence base and improved financial and technical management.

Figure 2 illustrates how the components of the project respond to the identified climate change hazards (SANBI, 2024).

33. The main assumptions underlying the proposed project are:

* Local engagement will be underpinned by project staff and non-governmental organisations (NGOs) towards community empowerment and capacity building across landscapes to maximise Eco-DRR benefits.
* Major national and sub-national governments will institute support cross-sectoral and collaborative approaches for addressing key policy, planning and institutional challenges.
* Long-term sustainability of the project is achieved through Eco-DRR with the enabling environment and institutional capacities strengthened at national, provincial and local levels; this will enable the development of a culture of Eco-DRR.
* Private and public sector investors actively engage with the project to recognise the benefits that can be accrued from improved ecosystem management and Eco-DRR initiatives.

## Theory of change (ToC) provided in the figure below.

### Goal
- **Objective**: To promote a paradigm shift towards low-carbon and climate-resilient development pathways, with particular emphasis on reducing greenhouse gas emissions from industrial sources through improved energy efficiency, renewable energy, and sustainable land use management.
- **Outcome**: The achievement of the goal will be measured by the extent to which the project contributes to the reduction in greenhouse gas emissions from industrial sources.

### Components
1. **Component 1: Strengthening institutional capacity for climate change mitigation**
   - **Activities**:
     - Establish and strengthen national, sub-national, and local institutions responsible for climate change mitigation.
     - Develop and implement policies and strategies that promote low-carbon development pathways.
2. **Component 2: Enhancing the use of renewable energy sources in industrial sectors**
   - **Activities**:
     - Promote the adoption of renewable energy technologies in industrial processes.
     - Implement energy efficiency measures to reduce greenhouse gas emissions from industrial activities.

### Activities
1. **Activity 1**: Establish and strengthen national, sub-national, and local institutions responsible for climate change mitigation.
2. **Activity 2**: Develop and implement policies and strategies that promote low-carbon development pathways.
3. **Activity 3**: Promote the adoption of renewable energy technologies in industrial processes.
4. **Activity 4**: Implement energy efficiency measures to reduce greenhouse gas emissions from industrial activities.

### Project Outputs
1. **Output 1**: Strengthened institutional capacity for climate change mitigation at national, sub-national, and local levels.
2. **Output 2**: Enhanced use of renewable energy sources in industrial sectors.
3. **Output 3**: Reduced greenhouse gas emissions from industrial activities through improved energy efficiency.

### Project Outcomes
- **Outcome 1**: Strengthened institutional capacity for climate change mitigation at national, sub-national, and local levels.
- **Outcome 2**: Enhanced use of renewable energy sources in industrial sectors.
- **Outcome 3**: Reduced greenhouse gas emissions from industrial activities through improved energy efficiency.

### B.3 Project/programme description (max. 2500 words, approximately 5 pages)

#### Project title: Eco-DRR

The project will be implemented through three interconnected Outcomes, in four DMAs and across four Provinces in South Africa.

This document provides a high-level overview of the project (including a climate risk and vulnerability assessment) supported by a detailed project design. The project design includes an assessment of the revising institutional and enabling environmental conditions for adaptation to climate change, using the climate rationale, identification of vulnerable communities, alignment with existing policies and strategies, and the enabling environment at a local level. More details on the selection of the DMAs can be found in Annex 2 (Feasibility Study).

#### Outcome 1: The incorporation of Eco-DRR strategies into integrated landscape management enhances the resilience of ecosystems

37 Outcome 1 focuses on the mainstreaming of disaster risk and climate change considerations into landscape management approaches, and the implementation of EEA and GEF measures in Alfred Nzo, Etzinger, Ngqo, Nkosi Makhanda, and Thembisa DMAs. The project will work with communities to restore degraded ecosystems and landscapes to act as natural buffers and provide essential protection services against droughts, floods, and wildfires.

The project will support the development of integrated landscape management plans that include climate change adaptation and disaster risk reduction strategies. These plans will be developed in collaboration with local stakeholders and will incorporate best practices for ecosystem-based livelihood options by looking at small, medium and micro enterprises (SMEs) and the Eco-DRR design. This will provide opportunities for youth to innovate and to support in developing sustainable and climate resilient futures.

#### Output 1.1 Ecosystems are rehabilitated, maintained and sustained managed for Eco-DRR

38 On completion of the project, ecosystems within the DMAs will be restored through rehabilitation and restoration of degraded ecological infrastructure and the adoption of sustainable land management practices, selected against agreed upon criteria in the Project Design Document (PDD) and as outlined in the EEA and GEF projects. The PDD is to be developed in the Operationalisation Phase of the project. SANBI will have the final authority in selecting the communities using the eligibility criteria, with inputs from stakeholders to support decision-making.

The project will work closely with communities to identify degraded ecosystems and landscapes that are most at risk due to land degradation and challenges, the ability to sustain these and the failure to assess the impact of these ecosystems on climate change adaptation. The project will also ensure that the rehabilitation and restoration efforts can become sustainable in the long term (van Wagen et al., 2022). In addition, past interventions did not employ a strong focus on climate change adaptation or disaster risk reduction strategies, which could increase exposure to hazards. As such, the integration of climate change adaptation into the Eco-DRR project design provides a new

79. This activity will demonstrate the linkages between climate change impacts and the condition of the natural environment. ## Outcome 1.2. Local, gender-inclusive and sustainable ecosystem-based livelihoods support locally led adaptation
- Resilient and sustainable economic frameworks for local communities will be strengthened through the introduction of new business cases in the targeted DMAs.
- The interventions are based on lessons learned from the pilot projects in the Alfred Nzo and Etheekeni DMAs. Successful EBA can potentially facilitate public-private partnerships (PPPs) to scale up these initiatives, with the aim of mainstreaming them into existing government programmes under government entities such as Department of Small Business Development (DSBD), the Small Enterprise Agency (SEA), and the National Development Bank (NDB). The DSBD, the SEA, and NDB will facilitate PPPs.
- EBA will be used to identify opportunities for local communities to generate income through ecosystem-based value chains and opportunities linked to green job creation. This will pay particular attention to gender equality and inclusion of vulnerable groups such as women, youth, and people with disabilities.

## Activity 1.2.1. Identify and assess opportunities to develop sustainable ecosystem-based livelihoods in support of locally led adaptation
- As part of the project design, only initial assessments were undertaken through engagements with key government officials and community members.
- The assessment will be based on lessons learned from pilot projects in Alfred Nzo and Etheekeni DMAs. This will assess their dependencies on natural, physical and social capital and their vulnerability to climate change impacts.

## Activity 1.2.2. Support the further development and/or establishment of small, medium and micro enterprises (SMMEs) for sustainable ecosystem-based livelihoods
- The project will identify existing business cases that have been developed for existing SMMEs in the targeted DMAs and reviving or refining them where necessary.
- This will be accompanied by the development of new business cases to support the establishment of new SMMEs. These will include providing technical assistance, training, and access to finance provided to take the business cases to market through sound marketing and supporting provider with engage with local business chambers and industry associations, and giving mentoring support to engage associated value chains.

## Activity 2.1.4: Update the Green Book

### 61. Develop and update the Climate Specific and Industrial Research (CSIR), the Green Book supports climate change adaptation in South Africa by providing an online repository of downloaded, baseline and future, municipal climate risk assessments, vulnerability indices, and adaptation action profiles for all municipalities in South Africa (GreenBook, n.d.). The Green Book is currently used widely and is a focus of ongoing municipal capacity building efforts. However, while hosted at the CSIR and supported in principle by Government, the system is not being updated regularly to reflect new data or information. This activity will update the Green Book with the latest data as well as building climate resilience at municipal levels. The critical updates to the context/data/information will reflect the most recent Municipal Climate Risk Assessments (MCRAs) and vulnerability indices, as well as adaptation action profiles. Once updated, more hands on mainstreaming and planning support will be provided to the four focal municipalities. This activity will also include the development and deployment of a mobile telephone-based application to make this data and information more accessible; what will be explored.

### Output 2.2: Eco-DRR is mainstreamed into national and sub-national environmental policies, management, and development plans

#### 62. National and sub-national environmental policies, management, development and spatial plans, and supporting instruments (e.g., municipal infrastructure master plans) are key to the successful implementation of ECO-DRR in South Africa. These plans and policies are recognized as important climate change adaptation responses. The translation of ECO and Eco-DRR into local-level policies and plans will be supported by this activity through a series of workshops with relevant stakeholders, including policy makers and decision-makers to mainstream EIA and Eco-DRR measures into policy, planning and budgetary processes.

#### 63. These workshops will also provide an opportunity for the identification of key actors (e.g., SALOMA, MDA and the DMR) who will be engaged in the development of these plans and policies. The roles and responsibilities of these actors will be determined during the Operationalisation Phase. Since SALOMA, MDA and the DMR are the lead agencies for EIA and Eco-DRR across sectors and departments, it is envisaged that they will be used to engage with them. SALOMA, MDA and the DMR will also be responsible for decision-making responsibility for the implementation of activities under Output 2.2 which will be stipulated in their MoUs. This will be determined during the Operationalisation Phase. The role of SALOMA, MDA and the DMR is to provide technical advice and guidance on EIA and Eco-DRR across sectors and departments. It should also be noted that a decision-making responsibility for NBI to determine its roles and responsibilities. Stakeholders will engage with the output and its activities through workshops and other means as required by the MoUs. SALOMA, MDA and DMR will provide technical advice in this regard. These stakeholders, including their roles and responsibilities, will be determined during the Operationalisation Phase (SALOMA, 2020). The same applies to the role of NBI which is also involved under Output 2.2.

### Activity 2.2.1: Mainstream Eco-DRR and ecological infrastructure principles and priorities into national and local environmental policies

#### 65. This activity will support the mainstreaming of Eco-DRR and ecological infrastructure principles into relevant policies, plans and instruments at both national and sub-national levels. The identification of key actors (e.g., SALOMA, MDA and DMR) who will be engaged in the development of these plans and policies is critical to this activity. These actors will also be responsible for decision-making responsibility for the implementation of activities under Output 2.2 which will be stipulated in their MoUs. This will be determined during the Operationalisation Phase. The role of SALOMA, MDA and DMR is to provide technical advice and guidance on Eco-DRR across sectors and departments. It should also be noted that a decision-making responsibility for NBI to determine its roles and responsibilities.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 19

## Outcome 2: An enabling environment is created for investment in Eco-DRR through a strengthened evidence base and improved learning and knowledge management.

In the DRR sector, there are currently no financial mechanisms to support private and public sector investments in Eco-DRR. In order to do this, it will be necessary to develop new financial mechanisms to enhance private and public sector investments in Eco-DRR. In doing so, this output will also contribute to ensuring a viable exit strategy for the project. This outcome will also focus on strengthening the evidence base and improving learning and knowledge management.

### Output 3.1: Financial mechanisms developed and strengthened to enhance private and public sector investments in Eco-DRR

#### Activity 3.1.1: Assess and strengthen the policy and investment environment to support private sector engagement in Eco-DRR

68. This activity will assess the policy and investment environment for private sector engagement in Eco-DRR towards addressing gaps and barriers to such investments and areas for improvement. In so doing, it will identify and assess EDA’s capacity to provide technical assistance and financial incentives to support private sector engagement in DRR-related risks and opportunities into financial planning and decision-making and emerging opportunities such as the South-South cooperation and public-private partnerships (PPPs). The assessment will be conducted with the support of the Taskforce on Nature-related Financial Disclosures Global and African Natural Capital Alliance. This assessment will also identify gaps and barriers to private sector engagement in Eco-DRR, and assess the frameworks that financial institutions use to assess biodiversity and climate-related risks and opportunities.

#### Activity 3.1.2: Build capacity and facilitate the exchange of knowledge for public-private sector investment in Eco-DRR

69. This activity will build on the findings from Activity 3.1.1 by convening a dialogue between government, the private and public sector by convening a community of practice for capacity building, learning and engagement around public-private sector investments in Eco-DRR. The dialogue will be facilitated through a series of workshops and roundtable discussions with key stakeholders. The activity will also support the development of a DRR portfolio and a potential investment mechanisms will be developed to support improved engagement and investments in Eco-DRR.

#### Activity 3.1.3: Public sector (including financial institutions) capacity will be built to understand the investment environment for private sector engagement in Eco-DRR, including the integration of biodiversity and climate considerations into their planning and operations with a focus on the adoption and implementation of the set of disclosure recommendations and guidelines for organisations made by the Taskforce on Nature-related Financial Disclosures and the Taskforce on Climate-related Financial Disclosures.

*For Output 3.1. GCF processes will be used solely for the capacity building of public and private sector players offering financial mechanisms to support investments in Eco-DRR.*

**Activity 3.1.1 is not contingent on the implementation or achievement of any milestones under any work undertaken by the Taskforce on Nature-related Financial Disclosures and the African Natural Capital Alliance.**

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 PAGE 20

## Activity 3.1.2: Develop a portfolio of financial mechanisms and investment packages and unlock new revenue streams for Eco-Friendly Private and public sector.

This activity aims to implement (with support provided to coordination) a range of financial mechanisms that will provide the necessary funding for projects under the Green Climate Fund. Parallel to this, existing vehicles will be identified and assessed to strengthen (where necessary) the development of investment options linked to outlined project opportunities. This will include the establishment of an Investment Platform (IP) as well as other initiatives under the banner of the DSDB and Small Enterprise Development Agency and Small Enterprise Finance Initiative (SEFI). The IP will also provide a platform for the identification, assessment and selection of projects for Nature for Wealth (NfW), Sustainable Finance Coalition (SFC), Conservation South Africa and others to develop fit-for-purpose investment packages. This will include options such as the establishment of business accelerators, use of incubation to progressively take businesses to market, providing support to enterprises in developing their business cases, and establishing partnerships linked to the identified investment packages that will then be developed that aim to attract both public and private sector investors into Eco-DRR. These packages will outline project opportunities for potential funders / investors and provide a platform for them to engage with the relevant stakeholders to develop the business case and undertake the process as these will be different to the sites set forward under Outcome 1. The criteria for selection of projects will be determined by the DSDB.

Importantly, this activity will also help to explore the business case and institutional options to establish a national public sector employment model that can support the rehabilitation and maintenance of ecological infrastructure. This will provide the platform for the long-term roll-out of development investment options, the implementation of incubators for SMEs and/or other entities in the private sector to access finance and support to scale up their business models, and to align with ongoing processes and to work with the various sector stakeholders who are working towards developing a national public sector employment model. This will be further developed after the period ends, with co-finance/par-take finance. The finance facility has a long-term vision and operates on a phased approach. The first phase is to develop a business case for the public sector employment model that will be used in conjunction with other entities such as businesses, DMAs, community groups, private sector/private sector to enter the incubator/access to scale up their business models. Once this process has been completed, it will be possible to scope out and explore the business case for such a facility, if no GCF is used it will be operationalized and rolled into downstream projects.

## Activity 3.1.2.1: Develop a sustainable public sector employment funding model to support the rehabilitation and maintenance of ecological infrastructure.

Informed by Outcome 1, a business case informed of an alternative and proposed funding model for a long-term public sector employment model that will support investment in environmental resources management and Eco-DRR will be developed. National government partners such as the DFFE, the Presidency, the Department of Cooperative Governance and Traditional Affairs (CoGTA), the Department of Rural Development and Land Reform (DARDL) and others are required to inform how it might inform relevant national processes. National government partners are required to provide a business case for the public sector employment model that will be used in conjunction with other entities such as businesses, DMAs, community groups, private sector/private sector to enter the incubator/access to scale up their business models. Once this process has been completed, it will be possible to scope out and explore the business case for such a facility, if no GCF is used it will be operationalized and rolled into downstream projects.

## Output 3.2: Informed decision making for Eco-DRR is supported and promoted

47. Informed decision-making on Eco-DRR will require the bridging of existing knowledge gaps, growing effective evidence-based approaches to understanding the costs and benefits and impacts of investing in ecological infrastructure, EBA and Eco-DRR. The project will facilitate a range of engagement platforms at local level to share information and best practices and to exchange knowledge and information.

## Activity 3.2.1: Strengthen and improve data management platforms, increase data quality management and develop recommendations for system improvement

### PARTNER ORGANISATIONS AND SERVICE PROVIDERS

#### Figure 3: Project Governance Model

1. **SANBI** retains final authority in matters of policy regarding the implementation of the Eco-DRR project.
2. SANBI, through the PMU, will take responsibility for managing strategic issues and then informing the projects operational team about these decisions.
3. The PMU will be responsible for ensuring that all project activities are aligned with national policies and plans.

The project's design will include the following governance structures:

- **National Climate Change Adaptation Committee (NCCAC):** This committee has access to, on either projects aligning with the environmental objectives of the NCCAC.
- The Project Steering Committee (PSC) is an oversight committee that reviews the work of the project to ensure compliance with project deliverables and GCF requirements.
- The PMU will be responsible for the planning, implementation, and execution of project activities. The PMU is supported by a Project Management Group (PMG), which comprises technical experts from various institutions and organizations involved in the project.

Tasks Team will support on thematic categories of work relating to the project. The Outcome and DM Tasks teams provide cross-sectoral and intergovernmental coordination and collaboration, noting that government entities are mandated to ensure that their respective departments and agencies are aligned with the project's objectives together with other stakeholders (more details can be found in Annex 21: Detailed Project Design Report).

In terms of procurement, all contracts will be awarded by SANBI. The PMU is responsible for the disbursement of funds and the achievement of the project objective and outcomes according to the approved plan. All projects working on the project will be governed by South Africa's Public Finance Management Act, the Municipal Finance Management Act, and SANBI's Supply Chain management policy and procedures for the procurement of Goods and Services. All contracts are aligned to the procurement regulations stipulated by National Treasury.

**Note:** No contracts will be required for the technical experts as these will be government officials from other departments.


---
**Page 26**
---

For the SABNII project, SABNII will be both Accredited Entity and the (only) Executing Entity and as such the governance arrangements have been designed to clearly communicate and a division of responsibility. No other Executive Entities or implementing partners are involved in this project. The project will be implemented by a consortium of service providers that will support implementation which will be finalised during the project’s Operationalisation Phase. SABNII has a standardised approach to the implementation of such projects that includes a detailed Operationalisation Phase.

The Operationalisation Phase will ensure that all contractual arrangements made are still fit for purpose and based upon the latest information available at the time of signing, as well as ensuring that all required approvals have been obtained.

Importantly, it is part of the SABNII policies and internal risk management approaches to not enter into any contracts or agreements with project partners until such time as the project has been approved by the Committee. SABNII will only sign off on a contract or agreement once the scope and location of interventions have been finalised.

SABNII will also ensure that all procurement requirements will only be finalised during operationalisation, once the scope and location of interventions have been finalised. A call for proposal will be issued which project partners can respond to. The selection process will be conducted by SABNII in accordance with the criteria established which will outline scope of work, roles and responsibilities etc. SABNII, as the Executing Entity, will oversee all procurement activities on behalf of the Committee. All contracts and agreements entered into will be signed off by SABNII through the PSC. Contracted partners will have some managerial or decision-making responsibility for their own interventions during the Operationalisation Phase.

SABNII will ensure that it receives funds into its own bank account. SABNII can receive funds directly from GCF without funds first flowing through a particular ministry or body in the government. According to Section 10(2) of Schedule 4, all public institutions and entities are required to open a separate bank account for their own bank accounts to carry out its responsibilities. Section 13(5) of the PFMA stipulates that funds received by a national public entity listed in Schedule 4 must be deposited into the bank account of the respective institution as approved by the Committee. SABNII will receive funds from the US$ bank account and deposit them to its GCF Readiness grant funds. SABNII will transfer funds from the US$ bank account to SABNII’s ZAR account as and when funds are expended or disbursed onwards. The project funding flows are given in Figure 8.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 24

## Figure 6: Eco-DRR Project Funding Flow

The GCF does not directly provide funds to projects but provides the funds to the Accredited Entity (SAANB), which will manage and facilitate the financial flows in support of project implementation. The underlying assumption is that the SAANB has a strong track record of managing financial resources, as evidenced by the Agreement and Terms Sheet. Co-financing will be provided by the DFTE and SAGA, using funds from the focus supplied by co-financing agreement (Figure 6). This co-funding will be provided through in kind support rather than cash transfers. The SAANB will provide a letter of commitment to the GCF for projects that are completed within the Operationalization Phase, while letters of co-financing commitment have been secured and submitted to the GCF.

There will be no direct transfer, or on-granting, of any GCF project funds to beneficiaries. All expenditure will be based on the approved budget and the SAANB will provide a letter of commitment to the GCF for projects that are completed within the Operationalization Phase, while letters of co-financing commitment have been received; it is the policy of SAANB not to enter into any contractual arrangements until project implementation has commenced.

There will be no subsidiary agreements, and all contractual agreements will be developed in accordance with the terms set out in the Agreement and Terms Sheet.

### B.5 Justification for GCF funding request (max 1000 words; approximately two pages)

The request for financing to GCF is premised on the increasing number of South Africans that are vulnerable to increased climate risks, particularly those living below the poverty line. Climate change impacts are already evident in the country and are expected to worsen in the future – thus reducing incomes, threatening food and water security, and increasing health risks. The majority of these people live in rural areas. In addition, South Africa also faces pressing development and governance challenges, seeking to maximize the socio-economic and environmental benefits from its natural resources while ensuring that they are used sustainably for adaptation to climate change. Securing the necessary financial resources to support the various dimensions of adaptation, particularly in the context of a country with limited fiscal space and a relatively small tax base is increasingly difficult.

While South Africa is considered as an upper-middle-income country, it has the highest Gini Coefficient in the world, at 0.623. Thus South Africa continues to face increasing pressures due to growing unemployment and political instability. The country’s social obligations are also high given its relatively low per capita income and a relatively small tax base. As a result, there is a significant budget gap as the country grapples with meeting its social obligations while ensuring that it can continue to invest in infrastructure development. This has led to a declining rating to sub-investment grade by major rating agencies), the fiscal debt rose, and financing of projects such as


---
**Page 28**
---

The ECO Fund has become increasingly challenging for governments. Gross domestic product (GDP) growth, which averaged 3.5 percent during and after 2010, has since fallen to an average of -1.5% between 2011 and 2014. Also, the country is facing a significant increase in climate-related disasters, which have had considerable economic pressure, reducing the overall availability of public finances to support the planning and execution of climatic interventions such as the Eco-DRR project. The investment by GCF is thus viewed as being critical for supporting the implementation of the ECO Fund’s strategy. This document proposes a new model that will leverage private sector finance to support Eco-DRR. The austerity measures implemented by the Government of South Africa have reduced public funding available for climate change interventions, which has been further exacerbated by low GDP growth rates in recent years. As such, this has significantly reduced the funding available to support EBA and Eco-DRR interventions. Noting that the country is facing a significant increase in climate-related disasters, which have had considerable economic pressure, reducing the overall availability of public finances to support the planning and execution of climatic interventions such as the Eco-DRR project, the GCF intends to develop ecosystem-linked value chains. This support will be catalytic in assisting the local economy of rural and peri-urban communities.

Estimates are that climate finance in South Africa needs to increase by three to five-fold from the current average of US$10 billion per year to reach a level of US$35 billion per year, which is required for the country to meet the 2050 net zero goal, and as much as USD 30.5 billion per year to meet the 2030 nationally determined contributions (NDCs) targets.

South Africa also has a high proportion of peri-urban and rural communities living in formal and informal settlements that are vulnerable to climate change impacts such as flooding and water scarcity. These communities, particularly women and children are often most vulnerable to the impacts of climate change as they are often more reliant on access to resources and services than men. Women and children also have a higher risk of exposure to climate-related hazards as they are exposed to hazards such as foods, which place both lives and livelihoods at extreme risks.

The quantum of GCF funding for the Eco-DRR project will be managed by the Direct Access Entity to implement projects in peri-urban and rural communities. The project will focus on interventions that address flooding and water scarcity. These projects will develop and demonstrate the interventions that will enable the project to make a material contribution towards achieving the NDCs, as well as the 2050 net zero goal. It is anticipated that the impacts of flooding and wildfires are forecast to have a significant impact.

The GCF has supported South Africa in implementing its climate change adaptation strategy through the development and implementation of various projects under the umbrella of the Department for Environment, Forestry, Fisheries and Agriculture (DEFF). Each of these programmes, whether at national or provincial levels, being responsible for ecosystem rehabilitation and restoration, with a strong focus on environmental management. The GCF has also supported South Africa in implementing a number of DEFF EPF programmes, while focused on sustainable environmental management, also seeks to address social development issues such as poverty alleviation and the empowerment of women.

Under this banner, the Workers for Water, Working for Wetlands, Working on Fire, and Working for Ecosystems have been implemented. The results from these projects are that significant progress has been achieved by these programmes at relatively small scale, much is needed from a financial and technical perspective to meet the NDC targets. # GREEN CLIMATE FUND WATER PROGRAMME PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 26

## The Green Climate Fund (GCF) and Water Programme

### Background on the Management of Ecological Infrastructure

The management and maintenance of ecological infrastructure, often referred to as a 'public goods sector' with an accompanying cost for private sector investment, has been challenging.

To address this, the DFFE in 2023 reaffirmed its commitment to protecting ecological infrastructure through the Working Group for Water programmes by allocating USD 152 million of grant funding over the next five years to remove HPPs and create new work on water-related projects. The provinces targeted are Limpopo (USD 84.7 million), Eastern Cape (USD 30.9 million) and KwaZulu-Natal (USD 36.4 million). In addition, the province of Mpumalanga was targeted by this project. Limpopo received USD 12.1 million, North West USD 10.8 million and Eastern Cape USD 7.5 million.

In 2023/25 this budget has been subsequently cut to a USD69 million (National Treasury, 2024). Furthermore, according to the National Treasury, the GCF is now targeting a 5% allocation for water-related projects in 2023/25. This is spreading at a rate of between 5 and 10 per annum.

### The Role of the Green Climate Fund (GCF)

This funding over time has contributed towards the development of funding gaps for such sectors despite these national commitments and apparent private sector innovation. Thus, GCF funding, coupled with state action and other public sector interventions, is critical to addressing this gap in a sustainable manner. It will enable investment development packages as well as providing support through accelerators and incubators for the development of innovative solutions that are both climate-resilient and Eco-DRR approaches.

### Opportunities for Municipal Scale Projects

The GCF has provided an opportunity for municipalities to access funding at lower interest rates than would otherwise be possible. This is particularly important in light of the high cost of capital, which can be a major constraint, especially for small, less financially stable municipalities where there often significant debt service costs.

### The Role of Eco-DRR Interventions

The combination of financial, technical, institutional and market barriers that prevent climate-vulnerable locations in South Africa from adequately building resilience to the impacts of climate change is well documented. In this context, the GCF has provided a unique opportunity for municipalities to access funding at lower interest rates than would otherwise be possible.

## D.1 Impact (maximal: 500 words, approximately 1 page)

### The ECO-DRR project will primarily contribute to ten of the eight result areas outlined in the GCF's Integrated Resource Management Framework (IRMF):

* **Adaptation Results Area (ARA) 1:** Most vulnerable people and communities; and
* **ARA 2:** Natural resources.

#### 11.7 In this regard, the Core Indicators 2: Direct and indirect beneficiaries reached and Core Indicator 4: Heaters of natural resource areas brought under improved management will be used to measure the project’s contribution towards the climate-focussed outcomes of the GCF funding provided to the project. Hence, GCF's strategic target for 2027 in terms of support for developing countries to conserve, restore or bring areas into sustainable management is being met by this project through its efforts to bring under improved and sustainable management 681,935 hectares of land under improved and sustainable management to reduce the impacts of droughts, floods and wildfires.

#### The project will be benefit an estimated 5,481,886 people (approx. 53% female) from peri-urban and rural areas in the three provinces of KwaZulu-Natal, Eastern Cape, and Free State. These beneficiaries are those who participate in project activities. There will be special attention paid to women and youth at intervention levels where access is limited or non-existent. The project will also contribute towards GCF’s strategic target for 2027 by providing support to the development of livelihood options; the focus on youth is given, important given the high rate of youth unemployment in South Africa.

Like the GCF 2024-2027 strategic plan, the project will in terms of its adaptation, the project will undertake interventions focused on improvement of early warning systems and disaster response preparedness in each of the four DMSs. This will include strengthening the capacity of the DMSs to monitor and respond to climate change impacts, including the identification of climate change induced hazards and the onset of potential disasters. The use of various technologies will raise awareness among communities on how to prepare for and mitigate the effects of climate change in their respective areas of impact in each DMS. The Eco-DRR approach will also be used for the development of adaptation interventions have taken part, will also likely indirectly benefit from the improved capacity developed locally through the above activities. This will be further enhanced as the project will be monitored and evaluated and the lessons re-assessed during the project’s Operationalisation Phase after more detailed baseline assessments have been undertaken.**

**123. The GCF 2024-2027 strategic plan places emphasis catalyzing climate finance from the wider finance ecosystem, amongst others through the development of innovative financing mechanisms (IFMs) such as microfinance institutions (MFIs), micro and national and regional financial institutions. Aligned to this, the development of innovative financing mechanisms will be a key component of the project. The project will support the development of new products and packages and the provision of business support. This will have profound impact on the number of indirect beneficiaries in terms of increased access to finance for smallholder farmers and other vulnerable groups.**

**124. A core aspect of the project design is the recognition of the importance of addressing barriers in the enabling environment, including policy and institutional frameworks, that are preventing or limiting the uptake of Eco-DRR as a means of adaptation. The project will therefore focus on strengthening these enabling environments by providing the basis for the paradigm shift that is required in South Africa. This includes working with institutions to support transformative change in policy and planning, as well as building Capital Institutions and organs of state. This is supported by the GCF 2024-2027 strategic plan which places emphasis on the importance of these two aspects will be supportive of developing transformational shifts in the manner in which Eco-DRR is taken to scale. As such, the project will focus on the following three core indicators:**

**• Core indicator 5: Degree to which GCF investments contribute to strengthening institutional and regulatory frameworks that support the implementation of Eco-DRR at national level (Annex 3).**

**• Core indicator 7: Degree to which GCF investments contribute to market development/transformations at the project level.**

**• Core indicator 8: Degree to which GCF investments contribute to effective knowledge generation and learning processes, and use of good practices, methodologies and standards.**

# Mobilisation of finance at scale

## Building on nature planning

### Catalysing climate innovation
- **Funding:** Evaluate new methods for valuing and incorporating ecosystem services through natural capital accounts.

### Mobilisation of finance at scale
- **Sustainable development goals (SDGs):** EBA / Eco-DRR
- **Public private financing**
  - Value added to sustainable business development including accelerators and incubators
  - Market sounding and business matching
  - Research, innovation and demonstration ecosystem management

### Coalitions and knowledge to scale up success
- **Partnerships:** Public-private partnerships, learning and training
- **Harmonised monitoring and assessment**
- **Data sharing**

### Exchange platforms, in particular, south-south
The mobilisation of finance at scale is a challenge that has been faced over the past two centuries in part due to changing conditions in the climate and landscape, as well as poor or expiring environmental management practices at localised levels. In addition, priority areas are vulnerable to compound climate hazards, in part, due to declining traditional knowledge systems and the increasing role of collective action in managing these risks (see case studies below). It is particularly important to note that as these peri-urban areas expand, they become more vulnerable to the impacts of climate change induced hazards.

### The potential for private sector investment
The mobilisation of finance at scale impacts beyond the target four sites by developing the business case for major public and private sector investments, as well as community buy-in, into rehabilitating the functionality of these ecosystems. This will be achieved through a combination of reducing the vulnerability to climate change induced hazards such as droughts, floods and wildfires. This will be achieved by quantifying the ecological and financial benefits of adapting management practices in the four sites (e.g., improved water availability and quality, reduced soil erosion, increased carbon stock, increase rainwater infiltration and support surface water availability and regulation, and reduce wildfire frequency).

### The potential for climate adaptive land management to build climate change resilience is currently not commonly integrated into public sector investment decisions. This proposal will seek to address this gap by engaging with private sector initiatives that focus on embracing adaptive approaches which result in increased community resilience as well as the broader ecosystem benefits. These efforts will be supported through engagement with local communities, governance agencies, and the private and public sectors by building trust, sharing knowledge and ensuring collaboration between government, private sector, communities, NGOs, academia and civil society. This will allow for a more holistic approach to addressing climate change impacts on land management in South Africa and beyond, activities across South Africa. Examples of these practices include the Umzimkulu Catchment Partnership in the Eastern Cape, which is led by the Department of Environmental Affairs (DEA) and supported by the Global Environment Facility and Rural Solution’s work in the East Cape province. These approaches would reduce the impact of climate change on land management through a combination of adaptive management practices that deliver commercially viable outcomes that benefit communities, private sector, and government alike.

### Each EBA site will be required to develop an investment plan
Each EBA site will be required to develop an investment plan which is expected to include a range of operational elements, the need for practice support and the capacity of local communities to negotiate appropriate financing mechanisms. The investment plans should also address the barriers and facilitate an upscaling of private sector engagement in the development of various and appropriate financing mechanisms.

### Assessing the policy and investment environment for private sector engagement
In Eco-DRR towards addressing climate change impacts on land management, it is important that the policy and investment environment is supportive. The identification and assessment of EBA and Eco-DRR linked financial mechanisms within the financial sector’s current integration of biodiversity and climate related risks and opportunities into financial planning will be a key focus area for this proposal. This will also include an analysis of existing financing instruments (e.g., green bonds, carbon credits, water funds) as well as the potential for shifting the investment paradigm.

### Lessons learned from the pilot sites
Lessons learned from the pilot sites under Outcome 2 and lessons on how to engage with local communities through the local site-based work under Outcome 1 will be shared nationally and be integrated into the approaches developed under Outcome 3. These lessons will also be used by the proposed ECF project to adjust government

### B.1.2. Project Outputs

* Output 3.0: 500 words, approximately 1 page

#### Under the South African governance framework, municipalities have a critical role in driving local socio-economic development. The Eco-DRR Programme is designed to deliver a range of benefits to beneficiaries such as those described above and the co-benefits that the Eco-DRR project will realise.

* **Co-benefit 1: Climate change adaptation**. Climate change adaptation impacts upon people in low-income and vulnerable communities, particularly women and children. The Eco-DRR Programme will support climate change adaptation activities, as well as taking of family unit. Climate change impacts upon women most significantly yet they are the least able to adapt due to their limited access to resources and decision-making power.

* **Co-benefit 2: Gender equality**. Traditional Leadership is a key part of the governance framework. Women are also vulnerable within pre-urban conditions, particularly in terms of access to land and water resources. The Eco-DRR Programme will support women’s empowerment by providing training on gender-based violence (Pienaar et al., 2018). Furthermore, women in these settings often lack access to sanitation and face discrimination when accessing services such as health care.

* **Co-benefit 3: Health**. The Eco-DRR Programme will contribute towards the achievement of Sustainable Development Goal (SDG) 15; Life on Land; SDG 2 Zero Hunger; SDG 3 Good Health and Well-being; SDG 6 Clean Water and Sanitation; SDG 7 Affordable and Clean Energy; SDG 8 Decent Work and Economic Growth; SDG 9 Industry, Innovation and Infrastructure; SDG 10 Reduced Inequalities and SDG 5 Gender Equality.

* **Co-benefit 4: Social and Gender**. Climate change adaptation impacts upon people in low-income and vulnerable communities, particularly women and children. The Eco-DRR Programme will support climate change adaptation activities, as well as taking of family unit. Climate change impacts upon women most significantly yet they are the least able to adapt due to their limited access to resources and decision-making power.

* **Co-benefit 5: Health**. The Eco-DRR Programme will contribute towards the achievement of Sustainable Development Goal (SDG) 15; Life on Land; SDG 2 Zero Hunger; SDG 3 Good Health and Well-being; SDG 6 Clean Water and Sanitation; SDG 7 Affordable and Clean Energy; SDG 8 Decent Work and Economic Growth; SDG 9 Industry, Innovation and Infrastructure; SDG 10 Reduced Inequalities and SDG 5 Gender Equality.

#### D.4 Needs of recipients (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page)

* With the adoption of the National Climate Change Response Act No. 72 of 2009 by South Africa’s NCA (2019) acts as a common reference point for climate change adaptation efforts in South Africa in the short to medium term, providing guidance across all levels of government, sectors, and stakeholders affected by climate variability and change.

* The NCA also provides a framework for Adaptation Action Plans (AAPs), which are required to identify and ensure adequate response to climate change. These include:

    * Reducing vulnerability through adaptation actions that protect ecological infrastructure vulnerability and building adaptive capacity.
    * Developing integrated climate services system that provide climate products and services for key climate vulnerable sectors and geographic areas.
    * Developing a national climate risk management methodology framework that integrates biophysical and socioeconomic aspects of vulnerability and resilience.
    * Facilitating the mainstreaming of adaptation responses into sectoral planning and implementation.

* Promote research application, technology development, transfer and adoption to support planning and implementation.
* Build institutional capacity and awareness for climate change responses.
* Establish effective governance and legislative processes to integrate climate change in development planning.

* Developing and implementing an M&E system that tracks implementation of adaptation actions and their effectiveness.

## 138 The NCCAS facilitates the degree to which development initiatives at different levels of government and business integrate and reflect critical climate change adaptation priorities, and thus inform resource allocation by the various stakeholders. It also provides a platform for dialogue between these actors on how best to facilitate and enhance climate change adaptation activities between different institutions and levels of government. These governance and institutional arrangements are important in ensuring that climate change adaptation is integrated into development.

## 139 While South Africa has retained its institutional capacity in some areas, over the last decade there have also been a steady decline in the number of staff working on climate change issues within the Department of Environmental Affairs (DEA). This decline has been most pronounced at the provincial level. The result is that the capacity to develop and implement EBA approaches is being transitioned into integrated built and ecological solutions. This requires national government, sub-national governments and business to work together in a more coordinated manner.

## 140 Phases of development are often characterized by fragmented institutional arrangements and lack of coordination between different levels of government and the private sector. In addition, there has been a tendency for complex technical, institutional and financial solutions to establish a new approach that is supported by EBA.

## 141 The rapid urbanization in South Africa is placing increasing pressure on infrastructure development in large metropolitan municipalities these challenges existed linked to very rapid urbanisation and the need to expand services. This is further complicated by the fact that many of the existing infrastructure assets are not well maintained, and the communities it serves is difficult in landscapes that are disparate and scalable.

## 142 The Municipal Infrastructure Grant that is provided to municipalities has a finance a range of infrastructural solutions that are often fragmented and do not address the needs of the community. This results in an inability for the communities to get for services as limited due to weak livelihood options.

## 143 In many cases, there is a lack of understanding among local government officials about the technical skills and competences to work with infrastructure and developed integrated solutions. In these institutions the understanding of the benefits associated with integrated approaches are often not well understood by the decision makers (e.g., the civil engineer and there is a dependency on the procurement of technical skills. With limited understanding of how to develop and implement integrated solutions, there is an inability for local government officials to make informed decisions in process and an inability to progress the solutions needed to build resilience. Building municipal understanding of the importance of integrated approaches and the role that they can play in developing infrastructure can be used concretely as assist in developing climate adaptation responses that are more sustainable.

## D.COUNTRY ownership (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page)

### 142 South Africa is a signatory to the Paris Agreement on Climate Change by virtue of its obligations in terms of the Paris Agreement on Climate Change by defining the country's vulnerabilities, plans to reduce those vulnerabilities and the required increases in ambition over time. The country has also developed a National Climate Change Response Strategy (NCCAS) that sets out the effects of climate change and provides a common reference point for climate change adaptation efforts in South Africa.

### 143 In addition, the country has promulgated its first Climate Change Act (Act no 22 of 2024) which guides the development of national policies on climate change. The Act also sets out the responsibilities and obligations of government and society for South Africa in the context of sustainable development. The Act recognises the urgency in addressing the threat of climate change and emphasises the need for an effective, progressive, coordinated and integrated response to the challenges posed by climate change. It provides a framework for the establishment of the National Climate Change Commission (NCCC), establishment of the Presidential Climate Commission (PCC), development of adaptation strategies and plans, and policy and legislative measures that will be required to address the impacts of climate change. The Act also prescribes a mechanism to support and finance South Africa's climate change response, planning and implementation by providing for the establishment of a National Climate Change Fund (NCCF) and a National Climate Change Implementation Plan (NCCIP). The NCCC and PCC are responsible for developing and implementing the NCCF and NCCIP respectively. The DRIP project can be instructive in informing the development of such a mechanism.

### 144 The country has also developed a number of climate change adaptation initiatives with a raft of ongoing projects and initiatives related to climate induced impacts, with a number of these also having valuable geographic congruency. These projects for which there are examples of both national and provincial level, have the potential to be developed as an integrated part of the project for greater aggregated impact, and important approaches to both the science and community engagement process

That will ensure the project. More details regarding the complementarity of the Eco-DRR project with past and ongoing initiatives can be found in Annex B (Feasibility Study).

### The draft Fourth National Communication (DNC) (DEA, 2018) to the United Nations Framework Convention on Climate Change (UNFCCC), the draft Fourth National Adaptation Plan (NAP) (DFTE, 2020) and the South Africa's NCCAS (DEF, 2019) recognise that climate change effects will lead to increasingly severe and frequent floods, droughts, heatwaves, and other extreme weather events. To address these challenges, South Africa has developed a range of instruments including the National Climate Change Response Strategy (NCCRS), which is aligned with the Eco-DRR project.

Furthermore, in recognition of the urgency not only to respond to climate disasters but to build and enhance climate resilient approaches to mitigation and adaptation, the established the Presidential Climate Commission (PCC) as an independent, statutory, multi-stakeholder body whose primary purpose is to oversee and facilitate a just and equitable transition towards a low-carbon economy. The PCC has developed a National Climate Change Response Strategy (NCCRS), which sets out South Africa’s long-term climate change adaptation priorities in line with the 2015 Paris Agreement, and its implementation plan. The NCCRS is aligned to the draft Fourth National Communication (DNC) and the draft Fourth National Adaptation Plan (NAP). The PCC has also developed a National Climate Change Response Strategy Implementation Plan (NCCRISP), which sets out how South Africa will meet its targets and objectives. Commitment to achieving the revised proposed target ranges of 389 tCO2e/510 MIO tCO2e by 2030 is aligned with the mid- and long-term climate change adaptation priorities in South Africa, including priority activities outlined in multiple national and sectoral climate change strategies, such as NCCAS, the National Climate Change Response Strategy (NCCRS), the National Environment Management Act of 2002, and National Strategy for Dealing with Bleaching Algae in South Africa. 2014.

### The project design is such that it works across various sectors and as such needs to work with the differing spheres of government and delivery across the spheres of government, and result in a single, strategically focussed “One Plan” for each sector. This will ensure alignment between the Eco-DRR project and other national climate change initiatives including DFF, COTCA, Department of Agriculture, Land Reform and Rural Development (DALRRD), as well as associated sectors such as Water and Sanitation, Energy, Transport, and Tourism. The project design reflects the importance of local level implementation and that key government departments and environment have been engaged together with both LLM and DM actors. These key stakeholders are aligned with the projects intent and are committed to support the projects implementation.

### 21 Both DLRRRD and SMRS are grantee entities that are required to provide cooperative governance across sectors and departments. #### Annexure A: Cost Benefit Analysis
To assess the cost effectiveness of the proposed project, the project benefit cost ratio (BCR) is used. In the economic analysis (Annex C), an economic cost benefit assessment was undertaken on the sites selected during feasibility and management planning phases. The results of these analyses are shown in the table below, considering the 50th and 90th percentile climate change projection.

### Notes:
1. At the 50th percentile, the proposed interventions and sites selected during feasibility deliver a Net Present Value (NPV) of USD 14.8 billion and a Benefit-Cost Ratio (BCR) of 1.94, demonstrating that every dollar invested yields USD 3.9 in benefits.
2. At the 90th percentile, the proposed interventions and sites selected during feasibility deliver a Net Present Value (NPV) of USD 6.5 billion and a Benefit-Cost Ratio (BCR) of 1.78, demonstrating that every dollar invested yields USD 3.4 in benefits.
3. At the 90th percentile, the proposed interventions and sites selected during feasibility deliver a Net Present Value (NPV) of USD 6.5 billion and an Internal Rate of Return (IRR) of 49.6%.
4. These metrics highlight that the proposed interventions and sites selected during feasibility remain economically viable and deliver value for money. The interventions collectively avoid damage costs of USD 41 million and 142 million at the 50th percentile, respectively.

### Impact of GCF funding
100 DFTE, together with DWS, has identified a number of projects aimed to improve the country’s water security and seeks to advance these projects. This will help to improve the resilience of South Africa’s water and biological resource to increased climate stresses. However, this is still not sufficient. In the past few years the country has seen a significant decrease in rainfall (approximately 20% below average) over large parts of the country, with an estimated loss of approximately 50 million hectares (19%) of South Africa’s landscape, will be a reduction of water flow by 14,444 million cubic metres per year, exacerbating the impacts of drought and hence increasing the risk of flooding. The current level of investment in environmental programmes in South Africa. This number falls short by 83% of the R12 billion required to make a substantial difference[2]. The environmental programme has experienced recent


---
**Page 40**
---

budget cut for the budget for the current financial year (2015/26) and for next financial year (2016/27) has been reduced by USD 73.9 million (Parliamentary Monitoring Group, 2014). This decrease in budget is in response to growing pressure on government resources and the need to reallocate funds towards other sectors of development that are under cut due to fiscal constraints. Private sector investment and partnerships in the sector continue to be limited owing to competing developmental priorities, a limited awareness of the water security risks posed by increases in climate risk and impacts, and lack of enabling environment for private sector engagement. This has resulted in limited or non-existent private sector involvement in many of the country’s environmental initiatives that are critical to achieving the country-wide impact so desperately needed. Hence, the urgent need to make the case for sustaining and growing investments in water security.

62. A key outcome of this project is to develop, strengthen and implement new models with the primary outcome of upscaling private sector engagement in water security. This will be achieved through a combination of public and private sector funding as private sector will need to be carefully facilitated to achieve this. OF take agreements will also be facilitated to ensure that private sector investment is sustainable.

63. Government is a necessary conduit for supporting pilot projects that benefit rural isolated areas. A developmental project model (e.g., the water security value chain) supported by public sector funding has the ability to leverage more private sector funding. However, this will require government to be able to provide a conducive environment for private sector engagement. The GCF funding will therefore be catalytic in developing a more financially sustainable model for ESA and the implementation of Eco-DRR. Without the GCF funding, the current situation in terms of sustainable ESA is likely to only get worse.

While there are numerous drivers and a range of strategic priorities that influenced the development of EEA and Eco-DRR, these were not always fully aligned. This project will work at national, regional and local levels to build the capacity of public and private sector actors for EEA and Eco-DRR and thereby incentivize investments in climate change adaptation projects.
