F. RISK ASSESSMENT AND MANAGEMENT

### F.1 Risk framework and management measures (inc. 3 pages)

The risk management approach is informed by general risk management practice and specifically the GCF Risk Guidelines (2019) with regards to risk identification, assessment, categorisation, impact evaluation and proposed mitigation measures.

- No interventions will be undertaken in any jurisdiction which is subject to or affected by United Nations Security Council sanctions.
- No individual or entity that is listed on any United Nations Security Council Sanctions lists, including the United Nations Office of Counter-Terrorism (UNOCT) list, will be considered for a counterparty, implementer, or beneficiary. Sanction screening will be conducted by the PSCM (with support from the FMO) in accordance with the GCF Risk Guidelines.
- There shall be no direct distribution or disbursement of funds to beneficiaries as support will be provided to communities through a range of in-kind technical and operational guidance and support, and businesses through financial services such as microfinance, insurance, and credit. This will also include market matching, as well as support to leverage both public and private sector finance.
- Before the signing of any contract, the PSCM shall review the risk management and mitigation of project risks that will be standard agenda items for the PSCM and the PMG, such that these are monitored on a regular basis. The PSCM shall ensure that all contractors are aware of their obligations to be accountable to maintain, supported by the inputs and reporting from the PMG.
- Over any period in which the project is subject to sanctions or other restrictions imposed by foreign arbitration against any Executing Entities in the Republic of South Africa, and this is not perceived to be a risk to the ESMF credit profile. The PSCM shall ensure that there are no any material legal or risk that will impact upon the project other than those covered by this due diligence process.
- The due diligence process that will be implemented for contractors will be used to manage conflict of interest, the declaration ensures that the evaluation and adjudication of bids is conducted independently without influence from personal relationships or interests. This will be done using a Declaration of Interest (SDS 4 as per South Africa’s procurement) which requires bidders to disclose any relationships or interests that could create a conflict of interest with the evaluation or adjudication of their bid.
- Transparency and Accountability: The declaration aims to ensure transparency and accountability in the procurement process, preventing potential conflicts of interest and fostering ethical conduct.
- Combating Corruption: It helps to combat corruption and ensures that bids are awarded fairly and on merit, rather than based on personal connections or relationships.
- Ensuring Impartiality: The declaration ensures that the evaluation and adjudication of bids are conducted impartially, without any influence from personal relationships or interests.

The due diligence process that will be implemented for contractors will be used to manage conflict of interest. This will be done using a Declaration of Interest (SDS 4 as per South Africa’s procurement) which requires bidders to disclose any relationships or interests that could create a conflict of interest with the evaluation or adjudication of their bid.

The purpose of the Declaration of Interest (SDS 4) is as follows:

- To ensure and maintain transparency, accountability in the procurement process, preventing potential conflicts of interest and fostering ethical conduct.
- Combating Corruption: It helps to combat corruption and ensures that bids are awarded fairly and on merit, rather than based on personal connections or relationships.
- Ensuring Impartiality: The declaration ensures that the evaluation and adjudication of bids are conducted impartially, without any influence from personal relationships or interests.

Regarding whistle-blowers, a Whistleblowing Hotline is fully functional and is administered and managed by SABNI. Advice on how to use this hotline can be obtained through the Chief Officer Operations, GRC and Management for investigation if warranted. The Whistleblowing Hotline can also be used to receive complaints from any individual regarding unethical or illegal conduct in relation to the project. The system allows for anonymous whistleblowing. SABNI also receives and administers formal, informal and anonymous reports of alleged unethical or illegal conduct through a dedicated email address, administered by SABNI’s Director: Governance, Risk and Compliance. The protection of Whistleblowers is included in SABNI's Fraud Prevention Policy. SABNI also maintains Hotline Registers of all reported cases and ensures that these are managed in accordance with the policy which is chaired by the Chief Officer Operations, adjudicates on alleged cases reported through

## Selected Risk Factor: Political flux

| Category | Probability | Impact |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Governance | Medium | Medium |

### Description
Both business and project sustainability are highly affected by a country's political environment and government policies, which are in varying levels of risk over the next decade.

### Mitigation Measure(s)
The project governance structures that will be put in place for this project will need to keep abreast of national shifts in policy and strategy as well as the governance and financial frameworks to ensure ongoing alignment.

## Selected Risk Factor: Oversight and governance

| Category | Probability | Impact |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Governance | Medium | High |

### Description
The oversight and management of a project of this scale that will work across national, provincial and municipal spheres of government is complex. The governance arrangements need to be robust in order to ensure the project entering the project life cycle at different stages with different roles, responsibilities, requirements and risk-management capabilities which has the potential to create levels of inconsistency in project management approaches.

### Mitigation Measure(s)
SANBI has a long and successful track record for implementing projects that required multi-layered project governance arrangements. In addition, DFDE has a strong track record in implementing a large number of GEF and other donor-funded projects across all spheres of government including the Department of Water and Sanitation, Working for Ecosystems, Working for Wetlands and Working on Fire. Further to the above, governance arrangements will be put in place to ensure effective oversight, monitoring and reporting of the Eco-DRR project.

## Selected Risk Factor: Environmental and Social safeguards

| Category | Probability | Impact |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Governance | Medium | Medium |

### Description
The Eco-DRR Project does not adequately meeting key environmental and social safeguards is a key project risk.

### Mitigation Measure(s)
There is a clear need to ensure compliance with GCF’s environmental and social safeguards and this is supported by the DFDE as the National Designated Authority, and SANBI as the Executing Entity (and Direct Access Entity) for the Eco-DRR Project. The DFDE has a strong track record in implementing projects that meet international environmental and social safeguards. In terms of these safeguards, the interventions to be undertaken would be categorized as low risk with respect to GCF’s safeguard policy. The project team will ensure that every intervention undertaken will adhere to, and report against these safeguards and regulatory standards.

# Selected Risk Factor 4: Foreign exchange

| Category | Probability | Impact |
| --- | --- | --- |
| FX | Low | Low |

## Description

173\. The nature of this application is in the form of a grant, and whilst the grant will be accessed in US Dollars, this will be translated into South African Rands (ZAR). The ZAR will be used for all projects implementation activities. Depending on the tranche arrangements for the grant – this could potentially impact the actual ZAR funds available for project’s implementation by SANBI.

## Mitigation Measure(s)

174\. The rand has consistently depreciated against the USD for the last 10 years (Barchet, 2020), and as such, the use of foreign currency in the projects will be managed on a conservative approach. Any significant changes that have a material impact on the project will be reflected on together with GCF management and programme implementation team. The project will ensure that all transactions are recorded in ZAR, and there is a record of all finances accessed and utilised under the project, so that there is a record and accountability for every dollar of grant received.

# Selected Risk Factor 5: Compliance (AML/CFT)

## Description

175\. GCF’s Policy on Prohibited Practices strongly discourages the negative impacts of fraud and corruption, not only the project’s implementation, but all entities involved. Instances of fraud and other corrupt practices, as outlined in the GCF’s Ethics Code, are considered a serious violation of the GCF’s policies and procedures. The GCF will rely upon the reputation of the GCF, DFFE as the National Designated Authority and SANBI as the Direct Access Entity and Executing Agent as well as the due diligence undertaken.

## Mitigation Measure(s)

176\. DFFE, as the National Designated Authority, together with SANBI as the Direct Access Entity and the Executing Agent, will ensure that all entities involved in the project are compliant with GCF’s policies. In addition, these entities are governed by South Africa’s procurement policies as outlined by National Treasury. The President has recently signed into law the Public Finance Management Act (PFMA) of 2019, which includes the Prevention and Combating of Terrorism Financing (CFTI) Amendment Act, 2022 and the Protection of Constitutional Democracy Against Terrorism Act, 2022. These acts are in line with the GCF’s Anti-Money Laundering/Combating the Financing of Terrorism Management Act of 1999 and Municipal Finance Management Act of 2003, for providing regulatory oversight and compliance to all entities involved in the project implementation.

In the development and implementation of the Eco-Dropt project, the procurement processes will ensure awareness of the GCF’s policies on prohibited practices. The project will also adhere to the cash balances that are provided by DFFE and SANBI. SANBI has developed and approved an AMI / CFT policy which complies with GCF’s AMI and CFT principles. This policy will be complied with during project implementation.

177\. Procurement processes for all entities involved in the project, including SANBI, must adhere to the Preferential Procurement Regulation (2022) that stem from the Preferential Procurement Policy Framework Act 2008. The GCF’s policies on prohibited practices will be adhered to and any deviations or exceptions to these approaches to variations, payments and processes linked to dispute and procurement.

178\. SANBI and DFFE will ensure that all entities involved in the project are compliant with their respective governance arrangements which minimise risks of money laundering, financing of terrorism or prohibited practices. These include:

- Due diligence assessments prior to appointment or the finalisation of any contractual arrangements.
- Ongoing due diligence undertaken on an annual basis for all contract holders.

## 3.1 In response to the impacts of climate change on the environment and rural communities, only through the leveraging of GCF funding will the realization of the Eco-DRR project be achieved.
This will build the foundations for Eco-DRR interventions, as well as support development projects where lessons can be learned to either replicate or scale up. In this regard, the development partners will have full access to the lessons learned with impacted stakeholders who had been in the intentions that either built on existing work or learn from new lessons.

## 3.2 The Eco-DRR project specifically, the PMU will align with national, provincial and local plans and objectives thereby promoting co-investment from government and other actors.
Engagement with stakeholders across all levels of governance is essential to ensure alignment between the PMU’s strategic plan and planned initiatives related to Eco-DRR. In addition, the involvement of relevant government departments, NGOs and civil society organizations will be critical in ensuring that the project aligns with national priorities as well as providing ownership and buy-in.
These governance arrangements will provide the necessary platforms for stakeholders to identify opportunities for co-investment that align with strategic priorities and planned programmes of work.

## GCF POLICIES AND STANDARDS
### 0.1 Environmental and social risk assessment (max. 750 words, approximately 1.5 pages)
The Eco-DRR project will be subject to the GCF’s Environmental and Social Management Framework (ESMF). The ESMF is in accordance with the GCF’s Environmental and Social Safeguards.
It is expected that the Eco-DRR project will have both minimal to no adverse environmental impacts, as well as minimal to no social risks. Overall, the Eco-DRR project can be classified as a Category B project with some elements of Category C. This categorisation is supported by the Environmental and Social Risk Assessment and associated Environmental and Social Management Plan (ESMP).

### 0.4 The proposed project will have an overall positive impact on the environment in terms of reducing flood, wildfire, drought and other natural hazards.
The ESMF provides a framework for the identification, assessment, management and mitigation of environmental and social risks and enhancing the positive aspects of the project through the project cycle (Annex 6). The ESMP will provide guidance that ensures that the Eco-DRR Project beneficiaries are committed and able to comply with GCF Environmental and Social Safeguards. The ESMP is developed in a phased way, providing the framework within which intervention specific Environmental and Social Management Plans (ESMPs) will be proposed.

## Table 8: Impacts and mitigation measures for Interventions – particularly those linked to Output 1.1

### Change in landscape and land use
- Visual impact of site
    - Good housing/amenity of the site
        * Good landscaping, engagement with communities will help us understand possible visual impact and how this can be mitigated and/or managed.
- Dust
    - Keep roads clean
        * Ensure that working limits onsite are kept to a minimum and below 20 hours/week.
    - Maintain groundcover for as long as possible to reduce the total surface area exposed to wind erosion.
    - Cover fine material stockpiles with non-porous water.
- Noise
    - Comply with Occupational Health and Safety Act (No. 85 of 1993) regulations regarding noise levels on machinery.
    * Provide training for workers and communities involved in implementation of the project.
    * Provision of personal protective equipment.
- Litter and waste generation
    - Daily litter collection
        * Separate waste into general waste (non-reusable, non-recyclable) and animal/animal product.
    - Use containers with lids

### Surface, groundwater, and soil contamination
- Proper management of any hazardous waste generated.

### Loss of vegetation / habitat and ecological processes / biodiversity
- Manage wastewater before correct disposal e.g. contaminated water discharged into a man-made wetland or treatment buffer around the site.
- Limit working areas to possible traffic flow way over other vehicles (including delivery trucks and subcontractors).
- Ensure that construction is done in phases.

### Labour
- Ensure sexual exploitation, abuse and discrimination procedures and grievance redress mechanisms are in place for all workers. Implement gender action plan recommendations.
    * Comply with Occupational Health and Safety Act (No. 85 of 1993).

### Community / Stakeholders
- Implement community engagement plan.

#### Community safety
- Undertake an intervention to limit dual exposure of these risks and develop a comprehensive risk management plan for the implementation of the Eco-DRR Project.
    * The management plan will form part of the project specific SEAH policy and protocol.
    * A detailed risk assessment will be undertaken as part of the Operationalisation Phase, if required.

The ESFM provides a capacity building component that integrates with the stakeholder engagement plan Annex 7. The GFM focuses on safeguarding of communities involved in projects and is required for GCF funded projects.

### GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 65

#### G.2 Gender assessment and action plan (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page)

**87. Women and girls in developing countries are often more vulnerable than men and boys to the impacts of climate change. They face a range of barriers regarding political leadership, access to information and resources, and mobility and voice. Equally important, due to economic marginalization, women and girls have less capacity to adapt to the consequences of the climate crisis first and worst, and women lack equal representation and power in crafting policies that affect their lives. Women are often responsible for water collection and other household chores; they consider women as tasks. Women and girls are most often the primary users, providers, and managers of water in their communities. In many cases, women and girls have less access to information about climate change than men and boys do. As a result, women and girls face disproportionate impacts from climate change.**

**87a. Women and girls are usually the ones forced to travel long distances over many hours to meet their families’ water needs. Women and children often bear the brunt of the lack of toilets and other sanitation facilities.**

**88. A gender assessment was conducted during the development of the full funding proposal to understand the underlying causes of these inequalities, as well as the barriers that women face in accessing climate change information. From the findings of this assessment, and an analysis of the proposed project solution, a gender action plan was developed. The gender action plan is based on the results of the gender assessment and will be implemented by stakeholders consulted in the field in the DMLA. The assessment undertaken demonstrated a sound legal, regulatory, institutional, and policy framework for integrating gender considerations into climate change planning and interventions at all levels.**

**89a. All assessments were conducted with the objective to understand the underlying causes of these inequalities, as well as the barriers that women face in accessing climate change information. The assessment undertaken demonstrated a sound legal, regulatory, institutional, and policy framework for integrating gender considerations into climate change planning and interventions at all levels.**

**89b. While gender-disaggregated data is limited at national level, there is a greater capacity of data at provincial and district levels especially regarding gender indicators on land tenure, participation in decision-making, livelihood activities, and access to resources. The assessment undertaken demonstrated that women are under-represented in quantitative and non-quantitative.**

**89c. Widening gender disparities are more pronounced in rural areas and poor communities, making them more vulnerable to the impacts of climate change, with reduced capacity, resources and/or opportunity to adapt. While the assessment undertaken found that few women held senior management jobs, although district development plans made reference to gender equality, there was no specific mention of gender equality or social inclusion. Gender equality and social inclusion are to be integrated into the plans. The integration of gender considerations is a gap that was identified to be addressed, in institutional leadership, into the future.**

**89d. Over time, women’s participation in decision-making processes continues to perpetuate decisions and programme management in project districts, and create inequalities in social relations and cultural norms.**

**89e. The gender action plan seeks to ensure women are empowered and enabled to meaningfully contribute to, and benefit from the project. The plan will be implemented through a multi-stakeholder approach that takes into account the needs, interests and vulnerabilities of women. The plan includes clear targets, gender-sensitive design elements, and specific measures for ensuring women’s participation and benefits from the project.**

**The project-level action of the gender action plan include:**
- Enhancing the capacity of the PMU to integrate gender considerations in policy formulation and operationalization of the PMU
- Integrating gender considerations into policy/programme
- Budgeting for gender activities
- Ensuring a strong, visible, and cross-CPO/DRF brand development and communications strategies
- Publishing gender-focused knowledge products
- Strengthening institutional capacity to integrate gender considerations
- Designing a gender framework and monitoring and reporting strategies
- Appointing a Process Manager that Puts the Functions of a Social and Gender Oversight Officer

### G.3. Project Implementation and Monitoring (page 59, 1 page)
#### Government Stakeholders' MoAs
1. SANBI will be the only Executing Entity for all activities and sub-activities in the project.
2. SANBI will enter into various agreements with government stakeholders to support the implementation of the project.
3. These agreements will include:
   - Roles and responsibilities, timelines, monitoring and reporting, and approval processes.

#### Project Implementation
1. The project will be implemented through a phased approach.
2. Each phase will have its own budget and will be subject to separate financial management and capital accounting procedures.
3. There may also be additional support required from SANBI, which can be provided by SANBI's supply chain management systems.

### NGOs - Catchment Convenors
1. In accordance with SGM processes, SANBI will issue a call for Expressions of Interest for Catchment Convenor activities.
2. The selected convenor will be responsible for leading the requisite capacity to lead the relevant programmes of work after which MoUs will be registered to support project implementation activities.

### Service Providers
1. SANBI will also procure service providers that will provide technical advisory support to the project.
2. These service providers will be procured in accordance with specific requirements as determined by the projects management team.
3. These contracts will set out scope of anticipated work and remuneration arrangements for each service provider.

### Interventions
1. Interventions may require additional support and request assistance from SANBI.
2. This may be provided by SANBI's supply chain systems, where service providers will be procured using SANBI's supply chain management systems.

## Accounting and Auditing
1. The project will adopt SANBI's accounting systems and will be audited independently (auditors selected through a competitive process).
2. Terms of Reference are approved by the SANBI Finance Unit on a yearly basis.
3. Equipment and improper usage:
   - Use of the SANBI supply chain system, protocols and contractual arrangements will ensure that all equipment, materials or technology to be used appropriately for the purpose for which they were intended.

# Secretariat’s assessment of FP267

## Proposal name: Scaling up ecosystem-based approaches to managing climate-intensified disaster risks in vulnerable regions of South Africa (‘Eco-DRR’)

### Accredited entity: South African National Biodiversity Institute (SANBI)
### Country: South Africa
### Project size: Small

## I. Overall assessment of the Secretariat

The funding proposal is presented to the Board for consideration with the following remarks:

| Strengths | Points of caution |
| --- | --- |
| This is the first project submitted by the South African National Biodiversity Institute, as a direct access entity, to the Board for approval. The funding proposal originated through the GCF’s Preparatory Support Programme grant and was tailored with technical support from the Project Preparation Facility. | The success of the project relies on strong engagement at national, subnational, regional/subregional, and local stakeholders. However, while engaging multiple partners is a strength in this particular project initiative, it also presents valuable opportunities for alignment and synergy. |
| The Department of Forestry, Fisheries and the Environment (DFFE) has been the national designated authority to GCF, closely collaborated with the South African National Biodiversity Institute on this proposed project. A strong alignment with national strategies, combined with a high level of stakeholder engagement and government support ensures significant country ownership and institutionalization. |  |
| The “Scaling up ecosystem-based approaches to managing climate-intensified disaster risks in vulnerable regions of South Africa” (Eco DRR project) aims to scale up ecosystem-based solutions for climate-resilient disaster risk reduction. It also aims to directly contribute towards the results of the updated Strategic Plan for the GCF 2024-2027: specifically, target 5 (TS) related to ecosystem-based adaptation, conservation, restoration, and sustainable management of approximately 66,010 ha of land. The Eco |  |

The Department of Forestry, Fisheries and the Environment (DFFE) has been the national designated authority to GCF, closely collaborated with the South African National Biodiversity Institute on this proposed project.


---
**Page 81**
---

## Strengths

* DDR project further aims to contribute to target 1 through its input towards South Africa’s nationally determined contributions, national adaptation plan and other targets - T2, T3, TS, T9, and T10.
* The Eco-DRR project is a broader project designed to actively engage communities, non-governmental organizations, government departments, the public and private institutions so as to strengthen the resilience of human infrastructure and climate-vulnerable communities, while creating an enabling environment to scale up ecosystem-based disaster risk reduction.

## Points of caution

The Board may wish to consider approving this funding proposal in accordance with the terms listed in annex II to document GCF/K/02/01, and if considered appropriate, subject to the conditions set out in annex II to document GCF/K/02/01.


# Summary of the Secretary's assessment

## Project background

* South Africa faces significant climate change challenges, including increased temperatures, altered rainfall patterns and more frequent extreme weather events. These changes threaten water resources, agriculture, biodiversity and livelihood. Major climate hazards include droughts, floods, heatwaves, wildfires and storms, which have intensified significantly over the past five decades.
* Climate change models project that these trends will not only continue but also intensify in future years. The impacts of climate change are already causing damage to both built and ecological infrastructure. They also severely affect local livelihoods through reduced agricultural productivity, increased water scarcity, loss of biodiversity, vulnerability of croplands and rangelands due to erosion, acidification, and soil nutrient loss; and a diminished capacity for natural ecosystems to provide ecosystem services.
* The country still faces limited coordination and capacity to implement ecosystem-based adaptation (EbA) interventions at scale and lacks adequate resources and inclusive community engagement.

* Climate change has been further intensified by human activities such as overgrazing, the spread of invasive alien species, deforestation and urbanization. These activities are perpetuating the cycle of land degradation in rural South Africa. Addressing these challenges is particularly urgent.
* To address these challenges, EbA and ecosystem-based disaster risk reduction (Eco-DRR) offer integrated, nature-based solutions that restore and protect ecological infrastructure, which reduces vulnerability to climate change impacts. These solutions also help combat land degradation, support sustainable livelihoods and strengthen community capacity. This is precisely the goal of the project: "Scaling up ecosystem-based approaches to managing climate-intensified disaster risks in vulnerable regions of South Africa" (Eco-DRR project).


---

# The ECO-DRR Project aims to scale up EBA and Eco-DRR approaches to manage climate-intensified disaster risks in targeted regions of South Africa.

By mainstreaming these approaches, the project will strengthen the resilience of ecological infrastructure and vulnerable communities against climate change impacts. This document outlines key aspects of the project's budget and components.

## 2.2 Component-by-component analysis

### Outcome 1: The incorporation of Eco-DRR strategies into integrated landscape management enhances the resilience of ecological infrastructure and climate-vulnerable communities (Total cost USD 49,51 million)

#### Activity 1.1.1 Focuses on establishing local project delivery hubs, governance structures, and disaster risk capacity in two target sites: Alfred Nzo and Ehlazini District Municipalities.

#### Activity 1.1.2 Involves community-level engagement to co-develop a shared vision, set strategic goals, and implement action plans. This involves engaging local non-governmental organizations (NGOs) to support outreach, facilitate knowledge exchange, and strengthen community engagement.


---
**Page 83**
---

Activities 1.1.3 and 1.1.5 focus on collaborative rehabilitation, management, and protection of critical ecological infrastructure assets. These activities, led by local NGOs, will incorporate traditional knowledge, engage communities, and scale up nature-based interventions to improve water quality, enhance biodiversity, restore degraded upper catchment landscapes, and improving rangeland management practices to reduce soil erosion and increase carbon sequestration. The project will also promote ecosystem condition and preserving ecosystem goods and services across 680/10 ha of land; and

Activities 1.1.4 will focus on developing innovative Eco-DRR tools to support adaptive management of site-based interventions. These tools will be developed in close collaboration with the Department of Water and Sanitation, Forestry and Environment (DFFE), drawing on lessons from ongoing initiatives and national programmes.

Under Output 1.2.1, the project focuses on promoting local, gender-inclusive and sustainable ecosystem-based livelihoods to support local-led adaptation through the following methods:

(a) Activity 1.2.1 involves identifying and assessing opportunities to develop sustainable Eco-DRR tools in collaboration with DFFE and other relevant stakeholders;

(b) Under Activity 1.2.2, a complementary focus is placed on supporting the development and growth of MSMEs linked to ecosystem-based approaches; and

(c) For both activities, the project will engage key government departments such as the Department of Small Business Development, the Small Enterprise Development Agency, and the Small Enterprise Finance Agency to explore clear pathways for private sector investment in Eco-DRR.

Outcome 2. The incorporation of Eco-DRR into transformative disaster preparedness and response reduces the adverse impacts of climate change on infrastructure and climate-adverse livelihoods (Total cost USD 9.7 million; GEF co-fund USD 10.2 million)

Outcome 2 focuses on building the resilience of communities and essential infrastructure through the development, implementation, and scaling up of Eco-DRR activities. The initiative will support the design and execution of inclusive strategies, plans, and response actions that integrate Eco-DRR principles. This activity will be led by the South African Local Government Association (SALGA) in collaboration with other relevant stakeholders such as local government, other collaborators such as National Business Initiative's Technical Assistance, Mentorship and Development Unit (TADU), and the Department of Rural Development and Land Reform to involve some of the activities under this outcome.

Output 2.1 focuses on four main activities to strengthen the capacity of the targeted districts to better understand climate risks and adapt to a more proactive response to climate hazards through Eco-DRR:

Activity 2.1.1 will assess the current situation, challenges and gaps providing information about the climate hazards in all four district municipalities and investigate possible solutions to improve the proper dissemination of the information.

Activity 2.1.2 will focus on building capacity for disaster risk management to build capacity and integrate Eco-DRR into disaster risk management plans;

Activity 2.1.3 will introduce innovative tools and technologies to improve early warning dissemination, including mobile apps; and

Activity 2.1.4 will update the Green Book (an online planning support tool that provides quantitative evidence on the impact that climate change and urbanization will have in South Africa) with new data to support climate adaptation planning and explore mobile app access for wider use.


---
**Page 84**
---

### Under Activity 2.1.2: Focuses on mainstreaming Eco-DRR into national and subnational disaster risk management environmental policy and spatial planning through the following activities:

#### (a) Activity 2.1.2.1 assesses the vulnerability of built infrastructure and settlements to climate hazards in target district municipalities, and identifies how ecological infrastructure can reduce this vulnerability. This information is incorporated into infrastructure plans and to identify potential funding sources; and

#### (b) Activity 2.1.2.2 mainstreams Eco-DRR and ecological infrastructure into national and local disaster risk management policies and spatial planning through the development of policy alignment, and the development of practical tools and guidelines.

### Under Activity 3.1: Focuses on strengthening financial mechanisms to support Eco-DRR through a strengthened evidence base and improved learning and knowledge management (Total cost: USD 87.7 million; GCF cost: USD 72.9 million):

#### (a) Under Objective 3.1, activity 3.1.1 will develop the policy and investment landscape for private-sector engagement in Eco-DRR and identify gaps, opportunities, and financial mechanisms that can support this engagement.

#### (b) Activity 3.1.2 will build public and private-sector capacity through a community of practice, and develop a strong business case and knowledge tools to encourage investments.

#### (c) Activity 3.1.3 will develop and test financial mechanisms and investment packages to unlock new funding streams for Eco-DRR, and explore the establishment of a national financing mechanism for Eco-DRR.

#### (d) Activity 3.1.4 will design a sustainable public-sector engagement funding model to support long-term rehabilitation and maintenance of ecological infrastructure, co-developed with the private sector.

### Under Objective 3.2: The following activities will be carried out to support and promote informed decision-making on Eco-DRR:

#### (a) Activity 3.2.1 will assess and improve existing Eco-DRR data management systems, develop data collection protocols, and establish monitoring frameworks to support integrated environmental-economic accounting for the assessment of ecosystem services. Data sharing and monitoring, evaluation, research and learning tools will also be enhanced using field data and stakeholder input.

#### (b) Activity 3.2.2 will develop an ecosystem service and biocarbon accounts using the System of Environmental-Economic Accounting framework to support integrated environmental-economic accounting for the assessment of ecosystem services and carbon accounting to inform sustainability policies and produce knowledge products to share findings.

#### (c) Under Activity 3.2.3, a social learning programme will be developed to capture lessons across the project and promote adaptive management through forums, working groups and events. The programme will also facilitate linkages with other institutions to conduct in-depth research and contribute to the Eco-DRR evidence base; and

#### (d) Activity 3.2.4 will develop a structured suite of tools and a communication strategy to support knowledge sharing across the project. This includes developing a project-level knowledge-sharing platform aligned with the social learning process.

### III. Assessment of performance against investment criteria

#### 3.1 Impact potential
Scale: N/A

The Eco-DRR project will directly benefit 366,089 community members from vulnerable and peri-urban communities of the local municipalities from the four selected district municipalities (Makana, Nelson Mandela Bay, Port Elizabeth, and George) in South Africa (46 per cent). The project also focuses on the 65 per cent involvement of the youth. Through project components, the project will contribute to reducing vulnerability and resilience against intensified hazards such as droughts, floods and wildfires. The project will also build the capacity of local communities, local municipalities, and regional and government officials to undertake disaster risk reduction (DRR) activities in their respective areas. The project will also help communities to explore opportunities for livelihood through Eco-DRR management businesses. The project will contribute to the achievement of the Sustainable Development Goals (SDGs) in the targeted district municipalities. This is due to the benefits received from the improved early warning systems and the financial benefits provided from the development of the financing mechanism under outcome 3.

The project will also conserve, restore and rehabilitate 680,110 ha of land, adopting the EbA and disaster risk management (DRM) approach. Through the implementation of the project, the project will also contribute to T1 of SDG-2, as the project addresses South Africa’s need for the food basket. The project will also contribute to T3 of SDG-6 by contributing to water security. It contributes to T4 on providing support to direct access entities through the GCF Readiness and Preparatory Support Programme grant as well support from the Project Preparation Facility. Additionally, the project will contribute to T5 of SDG-17 in terms of capacity building and support local businesses focused on eco-based adaptation and disaster risk management.

#### 3.2 Paradigm shift potential
Scale: N/A

The proposed GCF project aims to generate impact beyond the four targeted sites by building a strong business case for large-scale public and private investment, along with community engagement in the implementation of the project. The project will also help recover vital environmental goods and services that mitigate the effects of droughts, floods and wildfires.

The project will demonstrate the ecological and financial value of adopting ecosystem-based management practices – such as improving rangeland conditions, enhancing soil health, boosting carbon storage, increasing water infiltration, ensuring surface water availability,

### Sustainable development potential

**Scale: N/A**

The project contributes to Sustainable Development Goal 5 (SDG 5) (Gender equality), and which actively promotes gender inclusion by ensuring that women are not only beneficiaries of ecosystem restoration but also active participants in its management, directly benefiting community members being women. The project will help address existing gender inequalities and enhance the adaptive capacity of households and communities by empowering women.

In line with SDG 13 (Climate action), the Eco-DRR project responds directly to the increasing frequency and intensity of extreme weather events that threaten people's livelihoods and wildlife in South Africa. It applies EBA and Eco-DRR strategies to strengthen the resilience of ecosystems and vulnerable communities. By restoring degraded landscapes, wetlands and rangelands, the project enhances ecosystem services such as water purification which improves water security. It also builds local capacity and supports climate-smart land management practices.

Lastly, contributing to SDG 15 (Life on land), at its core, the Eco-DRR project is about restoring degraded landscapes, wetlands and rangelands in South Africa while improving ecosystem services. Activities such as alien invasive plant removal, wetland rehabilitation and sustainable grazing practices are aimed at enhancing biodiversity conservation and ecological connectivity. These interventions support the recovery of native species, strengthen ecological resilience, and improve water security for both humans and ecosystems. In doing so, the project directly supports SDG 15 by enhancing ecosystem integrity, preventing desertification, and fostering long-term biodiversity conservation.

### Needs of the recipient

**Scale: N/A**

South Africa faces multiple, interconnected challenges driven by climate change, environmental degradation, and socioeconomic vulnerabilities. A project like the Eco-DRR project can help address these challenges by restoring degraded landscapes, wetlands and rangelands while improving ecosystem services. This will reduce their ability to regulate water, store carbon and support biodiversity. At the same time, many rural communities face limited resilience to climate shocks. The Eco-DRR project responds directly to this need by strengthening the capacity of local communities in critical ecosystems, strengthens climate resilience, creates green jobs, and improves water and land management practices.

### Country ownership

**Scale: N/A**

The Eco-DRR project aligns closely with national and sectoral priorities, particularly in ecosystem restoration, disaster risk reduction, and climate-resilient natural resource

## Efficiency and effectiveness

The project also contributes to NDC 2031 as well as national climate change adaptation strategies, as it prioritizes EbA and Eco-DRR.

Moreover, the project was also co-created in close collaboration with DFDE, South Africa's National Designated Authority to GEF, through the support of a grant from the Resilience Fund. The project is subject to an independent evaluation by an external board, to ensure that its outcomes align to the country’s need for climate adaptation. The project will also closely engage with relevant stakeholders such as the Department of Water and Sanitation Agencies Association, National Business Initiative, Department of Small Business Development and South African Tourism Development Agency among others. The country ownership for the project is therefore very high.

### 3.6 Efficiency and effectiveness

The proposal presents a solid economic analysis to demonstrate the project's efficiency and effectiveness. The analysis covers four regions in South Africa targeted for ecosystem rehabilitation: Alfred Nzo, Elandskraal, Ngaka Modiri Molema, and Sekhukhune. It includes land rehabilitation, rotational grazing support, erosion control, and provisioning of ecosystem services.

The economic analysis applies the 50th percentile climate scenario as the base case, in line with international best practice. Under this scenario, the economic results remain positive, with a net present value (NPV) of R123 million and an internal rate of return (IRR) of 17 per cent, and a benefit-cost ratio of 1:8 exceeding all the 5 per cent social discount rates used to demonstrate the overall viability of the proposed interventions from an economic perspective.

The analysis shows that Sekhukhune has performed better than expected in terms of the performance of one region. Sekhukhune, which shows a disproportionately high economic return. The remaining three regions – Alfred Nzo, Elandskraal and Ngaka Modiri Molema – do not show positive economic returns under this scenario.

This shows unevenness of investment benefits across the target sites and the allocative efficiency of the project’s investments.

Furthermore, during the appraisal, it was identified that the economic analysis does not align with the actual intervention coverage per region, particularly in terms of hectares targeted for rehabilitation. The difference between the areas covered by the economic analysis and those significant and likely affects the regional economic performance estimates. The AEE confirmed this finding and recommended that the project should be scaled up to cover all intervention sites as well as expanded and built infrastructure in order to improve cost-effectiveness.

As such, the revised economic analysis has been updated based on these reflected in the current economic analysis.

It is recommended that the AEE submits an updated economic analysis once the interventions have been fully implemented and finalized. The following analysis will be important for confirming the project's overall efficiency and the viability of investments across all intervention sites. It should also be noted that the revised coverage will remain unchanged, as per the targets presented in the logical framework.

The project is fully grant-financed, with no cost recovery or revenue-generating components. The project has a high level of social benefit, reflecting the long-term nature of the benefits (e.g., ecosystem restoration, climate resilience, and support to vulnerable communities). The adaptation cost is USD 109.5 per direct beneficiary and USD 7.3 per total

### Assessment of consistency with GCF safeguards and policies

#### Environmental and social safeguards

**4.1 Project overview:** The project aims to enhance the resilience of ecological infrastructure and vulnerable communities by scaling up EHa and Eco-DRR approaches in four identified district municipalities covering four provinces in South Africa. The project has been structured into three outcomes: (Outcome 1) integrated landscape management enhances the resilience of ecological infrastructure and climate change adaptation; (Outcome 2) community-based disaster risk reduction and alternative disaster preparedness and response reduces the adverse impacts of climate-induced hazards on built infrastructure, livelihoods, and human health; and (Outcome 3) a conducive enabling environment is created for investment in Eco-DRR through a strengthened evidence base and improved policy framework. The project is categorized as having low environmental and social risks.

**Environmental and social risk category and safeguard instrument:** The project is categorized under the Environmental and Social Risk Category 1, which assesses the potential scale and general location of the interventions under output 1.1, and the vulnerability of the recipient communities to adverse impacts from these interventions. The project has a low or moderate environmental and social risks and impacts. Overall, the interventions are assessed to have potential limited adverse environmental and/or social risks and impacts that individuals and/or communities may experience as a result of the project implementation. These risks can be effectively addressed through mitigation measures. The identified potential risks, impacts, and issues are likely to be temporary or short-term in nature; they are unlikely to be significant or irreversible; (low) in magnitude; (site-specific), without likelihood of impacts beyond the active footprint of the project; and/or with no adverse impact on human health and/or the environment. The project's risks and impacts can be easily mitigated in a predictable manner.

**Environmental and social management framework:** An Environmental and Social Management Framework (ESMF) has been prepared for the project which outlines a systematic approach to screen, assess, manage and mitigate potential environmental and social risks associated with the project interventions. The ESMF will ensure that all activities are carried out responsibly and sustainably, complying with legal requirements, GCF environmental and social policies, and best practices. Environmental and Social Impact Assessments (ESIA) and social impact assessments with site-specific environmental and social management plans will be prepared for the interventions categorized as having moderate risks during implementation.

# Compliance with GCF environmental and social safeguards (ESS) standards.

The following sections describe how the project complies with the standards:

## ESS 1: Assessment and management of environmental and social risks and impacts

The project's ESMS has identified the following environmental and social risks and impacts associated with the project interventions:
- **(i)** air pollution from the use of diesel generators;
- **(ii)** soil pollution from the use of herbicides; 
- **(iii)** community health and safety risks, including potential spread of infectious diseases due to lack of access to clean water and sanitation facilities.
- **(iv)** loss of resources as the project interventions will be located on communal land.

The ESMS has identified a range of environmental and social management measures in the ESMS, which will be further detailed in the environmental and social management plan. These include:
- Training for workers and contractors to ensure adherence to the ESS.
- An exclusion list has been developed as part of the ESMS to provide guidance on what interventions that will not be considered under the project.

The progress of the ESMS implementation, including the development of the exclusion list, will be monitored closely by the PMU.

## ESS 2: Compliance with local conditions

All workers and contractors to be engaged in the project will be required to adhere to International Labour Organization, South Africa, SANBI and GCF labour-related policies and standards that promote sustainable livelihoods. Opportunities for community engagement and participation will be secured locally to ensure community involvement, with training to be provided by the project, as needed.

Local management plans will be developed in consultation with local communities to build relationships, enhance the development benefits by treating workers in the project fairly, and provide safe and healthy working conditions. The exclusion list will be used to identify risks that are not reflected in the exclusion list. Workers are anticipated to be exposed to occupational health and safety risks associated with the use of diesel generators (e.g., noise from machinery), rangeland management interventions, and activities involving typical construction practices such as handling heavy equipment or being exposed to hazards.

To address these risks, the project will implement various mitigating measures, such as implementing good housekeeping practices and proper site management. Engineering controls for noise exposure will be incorporated into the local management plan and development of emergency procedures will be incorporated in the site-specific management plans.

## ESS 3: Resource efficiency and pollution prevention

The impacts and risks are assessed to be localized and can be managed through environmentally sound methods and protocols, such as using locally available materials that do not require transportation nor consume large amounts of water or energy given the nature of interventions. Nevertheless, SANBI has identified a number of potential environmental and social risks associated with waste management, soil surface and groundwater management, dust management, etc., there are moderate levels of risk for these impacts.

The project will also be subject to the use of invasive alien plants. An integrated pest management approach will be adopted to ensure the strategic control of invasive alien plant species in the project area. In the event that herbicides are used as a control measure for invasive alien plants, if the use of herbicides is identified to be the most appropriate option for invasive alien plant removal, detailed assessments and corresponding management plans will be prepared prior to commencing any such activities.

The project will ensure compliance with all relevant national regulatory requirements and the lowest impact on the surrounding environment and community. The ESMS will be updated as necessary during implementation to address site-specific risks and impacts.


---
**Page 90**
---

### ESS: Community health, safety, and security.

The project interventions are identified as having limited potential risks with regard to health, safety, and security. The project seeks to minimize the spread of diseases through the promotion of rotational grazing which prevents livestock from congregating in one place for long periods of time. The risk that intervention sites are foreseen (i.e., potentially poisoning due to the use of hazardous material contained within or near the site) is assessed as unlikely. However, there is a possibility of conflict at the intervention sites being assessed as unlikely. The project will actively engage with communities and stakeholders in order to address any potential conflicts through a grievance redress mechanism (GRR), sexual exploitation, abuse and harassment (SEAH) procedures, and other appropriate mechanisms.

#### ESS: Land acquisition and involuntary resettlement.

Project interventions will be located in communal lands where land acquisition and involuntary resettlement are not foreseen. The project will ensure that the communities affected by the project have access to information to allow for the co-development of appropriate strategies to ensure access to resources are not restricted as a result of project implementation. Project interventions will be subject to screening. Interventions that involve land acquisition or involuntary resettlement, and interventions that would potentially negatively result in access restrictions on resources will be excluded outright as per the exclusion list.

#### ESS: Biodiversity conservation and sustainable management of natural resources.

The project seeks to ensure that ecosystems are healthy through the identification, maintenance and improvement of management of natural resources. Biodiversity in the intervention sites is likely to be enhanced by the removal of invasive alien plants and increasing landscape connectivity for terrestrial and aquatic species. The project includes the removal of any invasive alien plants. Interventions that are asserted to have high biodiversity risks that could potentially result in irreparable damage to terrestrial, soil, and aquatic biodiversity. Including but not limited to: (i) introduction of exotic or non-native plant species; (ii) planting of any invasive alien plants.

#### ESS: Cultural heritage.

The proposed interventions are unlikely to be located in areas considered culturally significant by the communities involved due to their religious values or to contain critical cultural heritage. The exclusion list is used to be during further screenings will exclude outright interventions that negatively impact or harm cultural resources.

### Implementation arrangements.

The PMU will be responsible for ensuring day-to-day compliance with the project implementation plan. Program Managers and Sub-Grantees Officers and Social Gender Officer will be engaged to monitor, verify and inspect activities on the ground regarding ESF and GRM implementation. Contracting parties will ensure that all relevant personnel are provided with adequate training. The PMU will provide all relevant SANHI personnel, including PMU staff, relevant executing institution representatives, implementing departments and municipality representatives, contractors and community members.

### Stakeholder engagement.

Multiple stakeholder engagements and participatory officials (including but not limited to local level government officials, NGOs, and civil society), were undertaken while carefully considering gender and cultural aspects during the project planning phase. The following steps were taken: (i) identification of priority hazards; (ii) site selection based on a consensus-driven approach; (iii) agreement on the approach to community-level engagement; (iv) alignment with past, current and planned activities; (v) obtaining consent from communities in advance management space. The PMU will continue with the stakeholder consultations during project

Structural inequalities such as limited access to land, water, and financial resources constrain women’s ability to adapt to these challenges and maintain their livelihoods. Climate hazards like flooding and drought exacerbate these barriers by disrupting access to critical resources for women who are overrepresented in subsistence agriculture and caregiving roles. # Risks

## Overall programme assessment (medium risk)

The Secretariat considers the overall programme as medium risk.

## Accredited entity/executing entity capability to execute the current programme (medium risk)

SANIE will serve both AE and EE for this project. No other EEs will be involved in the project implementation. The governance arrangements have been designed to clarify accountability and division of responsibility. This is the first funding proposal submitted by the AE and EE, and the Secretariat has developed a standardised approach to the implementation of similar types of projects that includes a detailed Operationalisation Phase supported by the robust oversight and advisory mechanism.

## Programme-specific execution risks (medium risk)

Complex implementation arrangement: The project spans multiple levels of government and partners with different mandates and interests, which complicates coordination and risks inconsistencies in deliverables if not effectively aligned. To mitigate these challenges, the Secretariat will support the AE/EE in establishing a Project Management Group (PMG) with technical representatives from key institutions apart from PMU. The PMG will be responsible for developing project management arrangements, ensuring stakeholders understand their roles from the start. The project will also align with the Secretariat’s established frameworks, with regular consultations and reference group meetings to facilitate ongoing coordination, communication, and problem-solving throughout the implementation.

## Compliance risk (low risk)

The proposed project activities generally present lower risks of money laundering/terrorist financing (ML/TF).

# Legal assessment

The Accreditation Master Agreement was signed with the Accredited Entity on 12 July 2021 (the date of signature). The five-year accreditation term lapsed on 30 October 2024 but was extended by Decision R.87/18, paragraph (g) until the earlier of three years from the date of lapse of the five-year accreditation term or the end of the project.

The Accredited Entity has not provided a legal opinion/certificate confirming that it has obtained all internal approvals for the capacity to enter into and perform the project.

The proposed project will be implemented in the Republic of South Africa, in which GCF is not provided with privileges and immunities. This means that, among other things, GCF personnel may be subject to criminal prosecution and/or civil litigation and extradition in the country; or he has been provided with appropriate privileges and immunities for relevant GCF personnel.

Therefore, it is recommended that the Board considers whether disbursements of GCF proceeds will be made to the Accredited Entity in terms of the project agreement and whether criminal prosecution and extradition in the country, or he has been provided with appropriate privileges and immunities for GCF and its personnel.

The submission of the information set out in this section, it is recommended that any approval by the Board is made subject to the following conditions:

(1) Submission by the Accredited Entity to the Fund of a certificate or legal opinion, in form and substance satisfactory to the Fund Secretariat, within 120 days after Board approval, confirming that the Accredited Entity has obtained all final internal approvals needed by it and that it is in compliance with its obligations under the project agreement;

(2) Signature of the funded activity agreement in a form and substance satisfactory to the GCF Secretariat within 180 days from the date of Board approval, or the date the Accredited Entity has confirmed to the Fund Secretariat that it has obtained all final internal approvals, whichever is later; and

(3) Completion of the legal due diligence to the satisfaction of the GCF Secretariat.

# Independent Technical Advisory Panel's assessment of FP267

## Proposal name: Scaling up ecosystem-based approaches to managing climate-intensified disaster risks in vulnerable regions of South Africa (Title only)

### Accredited entity:
South African National Biodiversity Institute (SANBI)

### Country:
South Africa

### Project size:
Small

## Assessment of the independent Technical Advisory Panel!

1. South Africa faces multiple, interconnected challenges driven by climate change, environmental degradation, and socioeconomic vulnerability. The intensity and frequency of droughts, extreme rainfall events and wildfires across the country have increased markedly over the past decade.
2. These changes are expected to continue in the future, further intensifying and accelerating adverse effects on food security and water security and the livelihoods of South Africans, particularly those living in vulnerable communities.

### The goal statement for the proposed project reads: If ecosystem-based approaches are adopted as a strategic measure to mitigate the risks posed by climate-induced hazards such as droughts, floods, wildfires, and extreme weather events, then vulnerable communities that are vulnerable to climate change will be enhanced. BECAUSE transformed landscape management practices in these areas will increase the resilience of ecological infrastructure and enhance the capacity of vulnerable communities.

### The project contributes to GCF adaptation results area 1 (Most vulnerable people and communities) and 4 (Ecosystems and ecosystem services). The project has three outcomes:

(a) Outcome 1: The incorporation of ecosystem-based disaster risk reduction strategies into integrated landscape management to enhance the resilience of ecological infrastructure and climate change.

(b) Outcome 2: The incorporation of ecosystem-based disaster risk reduction into transformative disaster preparedness and response to reduce the adverse impacts of climate-induced hazards on vulnerable people, particularly women and children in communities;

(c) Outcome 3: The creation of an enabling environment for investment in ecosystem-based disaster risk reduction through a strengthened evidence base and improved learning and knowledge management.

---

In its assessment, the independent Technical Advisory Panel (TAP) considered the document package submitted on 1 April 2022 by the accredited entity and discussed it in a focus call between TAP, the accredited entity and the GCF Secretariat held on 13 May 2022.

4. The Eco-DRR project, which requires a USD 40,184,179 grant* from GCF, is to be implemented over a period of eight years. In-kind co-financing is provided by three national institutions:

(a) South African National Biodiversity Institute (SANBI), contribution valued at USD 2.39 million;

(b) South African Department of Forestry, Fisheries and the Environment (DFFE), contribution valued at USD 5.24 million; and

(c) South African Local Government Association (SALGA), contribution valued at USD 1.77 million.

SANBI, the accredited entity (AE) and direct access entry, is also the sole executing entity of the project, which will be implemented in collaboration with a range of organizations: DFFE, SALGA, and the National Disaster Management Agency (NDMA). The project will focus on sustainable land management and will implement activities under outcome 1. SALGA and the National Disaster Management Agency are responsible for implementing activities under outcomes 2 and 3.

6. The Eco-DRR project is to be implemented in four district municipalities, namely Alfred Nzo, Ehlambeni, Ngaka Modiri Molema and Sekhukhune (see figure 2). These were selected because of their high vulnerability to climate change impacts, alignment with other projects, and a supportive enabling environment at the local level.
